## SEVerA: Verified Self-Evolving Agents with Specification


## Guidance


### DEBANGSHU BANERJEE∗, University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign, USA


### CHANGMING XU∗, University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign, USA


### EUGENE IE, Google, USA


### MING ZHANG, Google, USA


### DAIYI PENG, Google, USA


### CHU-CHENG LIN, Google, USA


### TARUN SURESH, Stanford University, USA


### GAGANDEEP SINGH, University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign, USA

Recent advances have demonstrated the effectiveness of self-evolving LLM agents on tasks such as program
repair and scientific discovery. In this paradigm, a planner LLM continuously synthesizes agent programs that
invoke parametric components, including generative models such as LLMs, and fine-tunes them. However,
existing work has shown that self-evolving agent frameworks can exhibit undesirable behavior on unseen
inputs. Because these synthesized programs are often executed autonomously, the lack of formal guarantees
that hold for all inputs raises serious reliability and security concerns. To address this gap, we introduce
specification-driven self-evolving agent synthesis, which combines formal behavioral specifications that the
synthesized agents must satisfy on all inputs with best-effort soft objectives that capture task utility. We
propose Formally Guarded Generative Models (FGGM), which allow the planner LLM to bind each generative-
model call to a formal output contract. Each FGGM call automatically wraps the underlying parametric
generative model within a stateful rejection sampler and a verified fallback, treating model outputs as samples
from a proposal distribution. Consequently, every returned output satisfies the specified contract for any input
and any parameter setting of the underlying model. Building on FGGM, we present SEVerA (Self-Evolving
Verified Agents), a four-stage framework for specification-driven agent synthesis. In the Ideation stage, the
planner LLM proposes task-specific generative components by constructing FGGMs. In the Search stage, the
planner LLM synthesizes candidate parametric programs containing multiple FGGM components. In the
Verification stage, we prove correctness with respect to the hard constraints for all parameter values, thereby
reducing the remaining optimization problem to unconstrained learning. Finally, in the Learning stage, we
apply scalable gradient-based optimization, including GRPO-style fine-tuning for LLMs, to optimize the soft
objective while preserving formal correctness. We evaluate SEVerA on invariant generation for Dafny programs,
policy-compliant agentic tool use (𝜏2-bench), constrained symbolic regression, and symbolic mathematical
expression synthesis. Across all tasks, SEVerA achieves zero constraint violations while simultaneously
improving task performance over both unconstrained and state-of-the-art baselines. Our results show that
∗These authors contributed equally to this work.

USA; Ming Zhang, mingzhang@google.com, Google, USA; Daiyi Peng, daiyip@google.com, Google, USA; Chu-Cheng
Lin, kitsing@google.com, Google, USA; Tarun Suresh, Stanford University, USA; Gagandeep Singh, ggnds@illinois.edu,
University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign, USA.

Introduction
Recent works have leveraged the code generation capabilities of large language models (LLMs) to
automatically synthesize LLM-based agents, represented as programs, from natural language task
descriptions [15, 21, 52, 54, 58]. Given a user-defined task, a planner LLM generates a program that
invokes parametric models, including LLMs, smaller neural networks, and external tools such as
SMT solvers, and executes this program on user inputs to produce outputs. While this paradigm

#### is powerful, it introduces significant safety and reliability concerns. Synthesized programs that

achieve impressive performance on training examples repeatedly exhibit undesirable behavior on
unseen inputs. Modern self-evolving frameworks [11, 48, 53, 56] further complicate formal safety
verification by fine-tuning the parameters of models embedded within the synthesized programs to

#### improve task-specific performance. Consequently, any correctness guarantee established for an

agentic program must continue to hold after these parameter updates.
The absence of formal correctness guarantees has led to failures across a wide range of domains.

#### In program verification, agents cheat by subtly modifying the input program, for example by

altering variable initializations, so that the modified program together with the proposed annota-
tions passes verification, thereby inflating task accuracy [3]. In code repair, agents delete failing
tests instead of fixing the underlying bugs [55]. In agentic tool use, unconstrained agents violate

#### domain-specific policies, such as refund eligibility and booking modification rules, in 68–76%


#### of interactions [22]. These failures are not isolated incidents. Rather, they are a consequence of

evaluating and evolving agents solely using soft performance metrics on a fixed, often small, set

#### of examples. Natural language task descriptions and evaluation on fixed inputs are insufficient


#### to prevent such failures. Instead, we introduce specification-driven self-evolving agents, in which

every synthesized agentic program must satisfy formal hard constraints specified as Hoare-style

#### preconditions and postconditions for all valid inputs. Our goal is to combine the flexibility and

performance benefits of self-evolving agentic frameworks while ensuring verified compliance with
user-provided specifications on all valid inputs.
Key Challenges: We summarize the challenges below.
• Balancing Compliance and Performance: Any agent synthesis algorithm must ensure formal
compliance with specifications while simultaneously improving task-specific performance. Exist-
ing work on deductive program synthesis [1, 24, 42] provides formal guarantees but typically does
not optimize task-specific performance objectives. Meanwhile, popular gradient-based optimiza-
tion methods for LLMs, such as GRPO [40], and self-evolving agents [11, 48, 53, 56] empirically
improve output quality but provide no assurance that outputs satisfy specified constraints.
• Binding Model Calls to Formal Contracts: Generative models (GMs), although powerful, are
inherently unreliable. Additionally, generative models are used at different locations within an
agentic program, with each invocation serving a different goal. For example, one call may require

#### generating Python programs, while another may require producing symbolic mathematical

expressions conforming to a specific formal grammar. Thus, we require a mechanism to specify
, Vol. 1, No. 1, Article . Publication date: October 2026.

--- PAGE 3 ---

SEVerA : Verified Self-Evolving Agents with Specification Guidance
3
and enforce a formal output contract for each individual GM call in an agentic program. These

#### contracts can then be utilized to verify that the agentic program satisfies the user-provided

specification. Recent work on constrained decoding [50] can enforce context-free grammar–based

#### syntactic constraints and limited semantic constraints such as variable naming or simple type

checks. However, these works are restricted in the types of constraints they support and require
modifying the model’s decoding procedure, which limits their applicability to open-source models.
Moreover, constrained decoding strategies can reduce model expressivity and distort the output
distribution, producing outputs that satisfy constraints but degrade task performance [4, 39].
Our Contributions: We summarize our contributions below.
• We introduce Formally Guarded Generative Models (FGGM), which allow us to specify and enforce

#### local contracts on model calls using Hoare-style preconditions and postconditions defined by

trusted, user-provided oracles. FGGM operates solely on LLM output strings and is compatible with
both open-source and closed-source models. The FGGM framework provides formal guarantees on
model outputs while also enabling gradient-based parameter optimization to tune the underlying
model to conform to the specified contracts.
• We introduce SEVerA: the first self-evolving agent synthesis algorithm with verifiable guarantees.
The approach consists of four stages: (a) Ideation, where a planner LLM proposes task-specific
generative components through FGGM setup; (b) Search and (c) Verify, which together instantiate
component-based synthesis, in which the planner LLM samples parametric programs in a verifier-
aware language such as Dafny that invoke the proposed components and the language’s built-in
verifier checks that they satisfy the user-specified specification over all parameter values of the
embedded generative-model calls; and (d) Learn, where verified parametric programs reduce the
constrained learning problem to an unconstrained optimization problem over model parameters,
enabling scalable gradient-based training without sacrificing correctness.
• We prove that SEVerA is sound: any synthesized agent satisfies the behavioral specification for all

#### inputs and parameter values (Theorem 5.7). We further show that, under sufficient conditions,


#### SEVerA can construct specification-compliant agents that match or exceed the utility of the

underlying GMs, and that FGGMs preserve the expressivity of transformer-based models.
• We evaluate SEVerA on tasks spanning LLM-assisted program verification, symbolic math syn-

#### thesis, agentic tool use, and constrained symbolic regression. Across all benchmarks, SEVerA

achieves provably zero constraint violations while outperforming state-of-the-art agents in task
performance: 97.0% verification rate on HumanEvalDafny (vs. 86.9% for the best baseline), 66.0%
accuracy on GSM-Symbolic (vs. 44.7% for the best constrained-decoding method), and 52.6% pass

#### rate on 𝜏2-bench’s airline domain using Qwen3-8B. Notably, on 𝜏2-bench airline SEVerA even

beats Agent-C [22] with Claude Sonnet 4.5, a state-of-the-art constrained agent using a frontier
model. These results show that behavioral constraints do not merely enforce safety but actively
prune the search space of candidate programs and steer synthesis toward higher-quality agents.
2
Background

#### Notations and Terminology: We use Σ to denote the alphabet, a finite set of characters. All


#### LLMs output finite strings over Σ. We use lowercase letters (𝑥) to denote constants, bold letters

(𝑥) to denote strings, capital letters (𝑋) to denote sets, and |𝑥| to denote string length. In the rest

#### of the paper, we treat neural networks as a restricted form of generative model that produces a

single output for a fixed input.

#### LLMs: LLMs L : Σ∗→Σ∗are probabilistic string generators that, given a prompt 𝑝∈Σ∗over


#### the alphabet Σ, sample an output string 𝑦∈Σ∗. Typically, the output length |𝑦| is bounded by a

fixed user-specified 𝑛, i.e., |𝑦| ≤𝑛or equivalently𝑦∈Σ≤𝑛. Fixed-length generation L𝑛: Σ∗→Σ≤𝑛
, Vol. 1, No. 1, Article . Publication date: October 2026.

--- PAGE 4 ---

4
Banerjee et al.

#### is implemented by iteratively composing two high-level steps, repeated at most 𝑛times. Each

iteration samples a single character (sometimes referred to as a token) from Σ or the special end-of-
sequence symbol ⟨𝑒𝑜𝑠⟩∉Σ that marks termination of generation. The first step is the distribution

#### prediction step, defined as L𝑝: Σ∗→P(Σ ∪⟨𝑒𝑜𝑠⟩), which maps a prefix string 𝑥∈Σ∗to a

probability distribution over Σ ∪⟨𝑒𝑜𝑠⟩. This distribution captures how likely each character is to
appear next. The second step is the decoding step, defined as D : Σ∗× P(Σ ∪⟨𝑒𝑜𝑠⟩) →Σ ∪⟨𝑒𝑜𝑠⟩,

#### which draws the next character using the current prefix 𝑥and the predicted distribution. If the


#### decoding step produces ⟨𝑒𝑜𝑠⟩, the generation terminates. Otherwise, the sampled character 𝑐is

appended to the prefix, and generation continues. Henceforth, we drop the subscript 𝑛for simplicity.
Definition 2.1 (LLMs). For a fixed 𝑛∈N, an LLM is a bounded string generator L𝑛: Σ∗→Σ≤𝑛

#### that samples an output string𝑦∈Σ≤𝑛for input 𝑥.𝑦on 𝑥is computed recursively as𝑦0 = 𝑥, and

𝑦𝑖=𝑦𝑖−1 · 𝑐𝑖if (∀𝑗≤𝑖.𝑐𝑗≠⟨𝑒𝑜𝑠⟩) else𝑦𝑖−1 where 𝑖∈[𝑛], 𝑐𝑖= D(𝑦𝑖−1, L𝑝(𝑦𝑖−1)) and 𝑥·𝑦=𝑦𝑛.
Rejection Sampling: Rejection sampling is a popular Monte Carlo method for generating samples
from a target distribution 𝜋𝑡when direct sampling from 𝜋𝑡is difficult. Instead, it relies on a proposal
distribution 𝜋𝑝from which sampling is easy. The support set 𝑆(𝜋𝑝) (see Definition 2.2) must cover

#### 𝑆(𝜋𝑡), i.e., 𝑆(𝜋𝑡) ⊆𝑆(𝜋𝑝). Intuitively, 𝜋𝑝generates candidate samples, and the rejection sampler


#### decides whether to accept each candidate, rejecting those that are unlikely under 𝜋𝑡(details in

Appendix B). The rejection sampler guarantees that no accepted sample lies outside 𝑆(𝜋𝑡).

#### Definition 2.2. Let 𝜋be a probability distribution over Ω with density function 𝐷𝜋: Ω →R+.

The support set 𝑆(𝜋) of 𝜋captures all points with positive probability 𝑆(𝜋) = {𝑥∈Ω; |𝐷𝜋(𝑥) > 0}.
Self-evolving LLM agents: In this work, we focus on LLM agents modeled as programs 𝑓: 𝑇𝑖→𝑇𝑜

#### that, on any user input 𝑥∈𝑇𝑖, compute the output 𝑦= 𝑓(𝑥). Typically, 𝑓is written in a popular

imperative language such as Python. The program invokes one or more parametric models (formally
defined in § 5.1), such as LLMs, as well as other tools such as an SMT solver, to compute the output

#### 𝑦. All parametric models F𝑝and tool calls F𝑐are provided as a collection of pre-implemented


#### library components. We denote this library by F = F𝑝∪F𝑐(formal definition in § 5.1). In the


#### self-evolving paradigm, the program 𝑓is not handwritten. Instead, it is generated by a planner

LLM based on task-specific instructions provided as a prompt. The high-level goal is to synthesize a
program 𝑓that improves on-task performance. Given a fixed training dataset of input and optional

#### ground-truth output pairs D ⊆𝑇𝑖× 𝑇𝑜and a black-box reward oracle R : 𝐷× 𝑇𝑜→[0, 1], the

synthesized agent maximizes the aggregated reward

#### the ground truth 𝑦𝑖may not be available; in such cases, the reward is denoted as R(𝑥𝑖, _, 𝑓(𝑥𝑖)).

Specification-driven agent synthesis

#### Eq. 1 searches only for the optimal program 𝑓∗that maximizes the reward on the training set


#### D. However, it provides no guarantee about how 𝑓∗performs on unseen user inputs outside D.


#### In contrast, deductive program synthesis [1, 42] allows program generation to be controlled by

formal behavioral specifications. In agent synthesis, we allow users to specify Hoare-style pre and

#### postconditions Φ : 𝑇𝑖→{𝑇, 𝐹}, Ψ : 𝑇𝑖× 𝑇𝑜→{𝑇, 𝐹}, which any synthesized agent 𝑓: 𝑇𝑖→𝑇𝑜

must comply with on all valid inputs satisfying Φ. Both (Φ, Ψ) are defined with logical operators
over trusted user-provided checkers that are part of the non-parametric library component F𝑐(see

Although our synthesis problem shares similarities with the classic deductive component-based

#### synthesis problem [12], there are two key differences. In component-based synthesis, the goal


#### is to construct programs that satisfy a behavioral specification by composing components from


#### a fixed user-provided set. In contrast, agent synthesis can propose and fine-tune task-specific

generative components, thereby continually expanding the available component library. Moreover,

#### the synthesis algorithm must jointly optimize task reward and specification compliance. As our

experiments illustrate, many programs that satisfy the specification can achieve very low reward,
making them safe but ineffective for the target task. Next, we illustrate how practical constraints
from several application domains can be encoded as behavioral specifications (Φ, Ψ).

#### Examples of specifications (Φ, Ψ): We show encodings of (Φ, Ψ) for four different tasks from

distinct domains. a Program Verification: In this task, given a program in a verification-aware

#### language such as Dafny with a predefined specification, the agent is expected to synthesize an-


#### notations (e.g., loop invariants, assertions, ranking functions) [32, 35, 43]. Task success depends


#### on whether the annotated program verifies. Recent work [3] shows that agents using frontier

models (e.g., Claude) may cheat by subtly modifying the input program, such as altering variable
initializations, instead of producing correct annotations. They further report that such modifications

#### were not detected by prior string-based checkers, leading to inflated task accuracy. In contrast,

formal AST-based diff checkers that syntactically enforce equivalence between the input program
and the annotated output serve as a behavioral specification, ensuring the agent cannot cheat even
on unseen inputs. b Constrained LLM Generation: Constrained generation captures a class of
problems in which LLMs are required to generate strings that follow a formal structure. Typically,
these formal structures are defined using formal grammars (regular [44] or context-free grammars
[39, 50]), or static analyzers such as type checkers [36, 37]. In our setting, these formal structures
define the output specification Ψ. We consider the task of generating symbolic math expressions
from natural language questions, where hard constraints ensure that the synthesized agent always
produces at least a structurally valid expression. c Agentic Tool Use: Conversational LLM agents

#### deployed in customer-service settings must select and invoke API tools to resolve user requests

while respecting domain-specific policies, such as refund eligibility, booking modification rules, and
authentication-before-access requirements [5]. These temporal and logical policy constraints can be
expressed as formal specifications that govern the ordering and content of agent actions. Agent-C
[22] provides a domain-specific language for specifying such temporal properties and exposes an
incremental validity checker for tool calls. Given the current tool-call execution trace as input, the
checker determines whether the proposed sequence of tool calls complies with the specified formal
policy. In our formulation, Ψ uses the Agent-C checker to define a hard constraint on each tool call,
ensuring that synthesized agents never violate domain policies at any step, regardless of the user
input. d Scientific Discovery: We consider constrained symbolic regression, where the task is
to recover an unknown mathematical formula from observed data. The formal constraints (Φ, Ψ)
encode prior knowledge about the target formula, such as known symbolic bounds [7, 19, 30] or

#### asymptotic behavior [29]. Any generated formula that violates these hard constraints is invalid,

regardless of its empirical fit to the data. Moreover, in the presence of noise, the constraints (Φ, Ψ)

#### help prevent overfitting, which cannot be addressed solely by optimizing a soft objective [26].

, Vol. 1, No. 1, Article . Publication date: October 2026.

--- PAGE 6 ---

6
Banerjee et al.
Fig. 1. Overview of SEVerA, which operates in four steps. (1) Ideation: given the task information, library
functions F𝑐, and parametric generative models (GMs) F𝑝, the planner LLM proposes task-specific FGGMs,
each binding a GM call to local contracts (Φ𝑙, Ψ𝑙), a prompting program 𝑓𝑝, a verified fallback 𝑓𝑑, and expands
the library components. Steps (2) and (3) form component-based synthesis: (2) Search: the planner emits
a parametric agentic program over the expanded library, including parametric FGGMs; (3) Verify: the
verifier checks all FGGM definitions and the program against (Φ, Ψ), accepting valid programs and otherwise
returning an error to the planner. (4) Learn: the GM parameters within each FGGM are tuned to improve
conformance with the local contracts and to increase reward R over D. SEVerA maintains a pool of verified
fine-tuned agents, reuses their traces to seed new candidates via step (1), and returns the highest-R agent.
Overall, the hard behavioral specification captures the minimal requirements that must hold on all
inputs, while the reward function R specifies a best-effort task performance metric.
4
Overview

#### Fig. 1 gives a high-level overview of SEVerA for solving the constrained learning problem in


#### Eq. 2 over the search space S(𝐺, F). Unlike classical component-based synthesis, the presence


#### of parametric components F𝑝(e.g., LLMs) means that the search space contains infinitely many


#### components, one for each underlying parameter value. Additionally, the reward oracle R makes

finding a specification-compliant program alone insufficient. Instead, the optimal solution requires
searching over all specification-compliant candidates and picking the one with the highest reward.

#### SEVerA systematically breaks down the search procedure into four tractable high-level steps,


#### combining the benefits of both constraint-guided program search from deductive synthesis and


#### unconstrained gradient-based parameter optimization. The first, Ideation, step extracts a fixed

number of task-specific generative components G using models from F𝑝, thereby expanding the
set F𝑐. The Search and Verify steps then perform deductive component-based synthesis to produce

#### parametric programs over G ∪F𝑐that satisfy the specification (Φ, Ψ) over all parameter values.

Finally, the gradient-based optimization step fine-tunes the parametric models to jointly maximize
cumulative reward while complying with the formal contracts defined in the corresponding FGGM.

#### The proposed Formally Guarded Generative Model (FGGM) serves as the critical link between

these stages. During the ideation step, it binds each unreliable GM call to explicit local contracts
, Vol. 1, No. 1, Article . Publication date: October 2026.

  [本页图片: page6_img1.png]

--- PAGE 7 ---

SEVerA : Verified Self-Evolving Agents with Specification Guidance
7

#### and a verified non-parametric fallback program, ensuring that these contracts hold regardless


#### of the underlying GM parameters. The synthesis step then leverages these local contracts to


#### prove the correctness of the parametric program with respect to the behavioral specification.

Beyond providing correctness guarantees, these contracts also extract local learning objectives by

#### characterizing the expected outputs of each GM call. This enables the final optimization step to


#### improve each GM’s conformance to its local contracts, connecting constrained program search

with unconstrained learning. Next, we discuss the key steps of SEVerA using two representative
and easy-to-understand problem instances from scientific discovery and program verification.
4.1
Example Instantiation of SEVerA

#### Axioms: The axioms A encode known properties of library components in F, including their

Like other component-based synthesis frameworks [12], SEVerA assumes that all library compo-
nents have correct contracts, and that they are pure and terminating. Verifying the correctness of
the library components and axioms is outside the scope of SEVerA.

#### 4.1.2


#### LLM-Assisted Automated Verification. This task aims to synthesize annotations, such as


#### inductive loop invariants, ranking functions, and assertions, that enable automatic verification

and termination checking of input Dafny programs with pre-encoded input–output specifications

#### [32, 43]. The agent has type signature (𝑝: 𝑠𝑡𝑟) →𝑠𝑡𝑟, mapping an input Dafny program with


#### pre-encoded specifications, represented as a string, to an annotated Dafny program. The point-

wise reward function R evaluates whether the annotated program verifies within a pre-fixed time
budget, assigning 0 to unverified programs and 1 to verified ones (Eq. 10). Hard constraints (Φ, Ψ)

#### ensure that, for any parsable input program, the output string remains parsable and does not

change the input program beyond adding annotations (Eq. 11). We use the Dafny built-in verifier

#### to define R, and the Dafny parser together with the AST-based formal diff checker from [3] to

define (Φ, Ψ). 𝑛𝑜𝐷𝑖𝑓𝑓returns True if the second input program is syntactically equivalent to the

#### first (ignoring annotations), and False otherwise. The library function set F includes all these

components. Although parsing and diff checks could be incorporated into R by penalizing invalid
outputs on D, these constraints must hold for all possible inputs, not only those in D. Therefore,
any candidate agent that fails to satisfy them must be filtered out.

#### Library Components: We use the same search space as constrained symbolic regression. F

includes the verifier with a pre-fixed time budget 𝑣𝑒𝑟𝑖𝑓𝑦, the Dafny parser 𝑝𝑎𝑟𝑠𝑒, the diff-checker
𝑛𝑜𝐷𝑖𝑓𝑓. We show the type signature and formal contract of representative functions in Eq. 9 while
providing the rest in Appendix E.

4.2
Formally Guarded Generative Model
The Formally Guarded Generative Model (FGGM) framework allows the planner LLM to bind each
unreliable generative model (GM) call to Hoare-style pre- and post-conditions (Φ𝑙, Ψ𝑙), referred to
as the local contract of that call. Once an FGGM is verified to be well-formed, its local contracts can
be used to prove the correctness of the agentic program with respect to the behavioral specifications
(Φ, Ψ). Our FGGM design ensures the following essential properties. 1 Flexibility: Unlike functions

#### in F𝑐, output contracts cannot be predefined by users because the expected output of a GM

depends on the generation goal and therefore varies across calls. In our design, the planner LLM
synthesizes custom quantifier-free formulas over terms involving non-parametric functions (e.g.,
sqrt) and predicates (e.g., 𝑛𝑜𝐷𝑖𝑓𝑓) from F𝑐to specify these local contracts. 2 Parameter-Independent
Correctness: Provided the defined FGGM is well-formed, the specified contracts hold irrespective

#### of the parameters of the underlying GM. This property is crucial to ensure that gradient-based


#### parameter optimization never breaks the correctness guarantees. 3 Local Learning Objective:


#### Although correctness holds irrespective of parameter values, the local contracts (Φ𝑙, Ψ𝑙) extract


#### the local learning objective for a particular GM call by characterizing its expected output. This

, Vol. 1, No. 1, Article . Publication date: October 2026.

--- PAGE 9 ---

SEVerA : Verified Self-Evolving Agents with Specification Guidance
9
objective can guide gradient-based optimization so that, after tuning, the GM’s conformance with
(Φ𝑙, Ψ𝑙) improves (§ 4.2.4).

#### 4.2.1


#### Construction. The primary building block of each FGGM is a stateful rejection sampler

defined by (Φ𝑙, Ψ𝑙) (see Fig. 2). The rejection sampler treats the output of the underlying GM as the
proposal distribution 𝜋𝑝, while Ψ𝑙defines the support set of the target distribution 𝜋𝑡(Definition 2.2).
It repeatedly draws samples from the GM and checks their satisfiability (𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘Ψ𝑙) with respect to
Ψ𝑙, rejecting any sample that fails the check. For each rejected sample, it collects the evaluations

#### of all atomic formulas and uses the rejected sample together with these evaluations (𝐸𝑟𝑟Ψ𝑙) as

feedback for the next stateful sample generation. Since Ψ𝑙is quantifier-free and the user-provided

#### components are computable, the checker can evaluate all atomic formulas and is therefore both


#### sound and complete. This setup automatically simulates the widely successful feedback-guided


#### generation paradigm [42, 56] for any local contract while requiring only output-string access.

Benefits of the stateful rejection sampler over the stateless version are shown in § 6.5 (Table 9).

#### For each FGGM with local contracts (Φ𝑙, Ψ𝑙) and underlying parametric GM LΘ, the planner


#### LLM synthesizes two key non-parametric programs using only functions from F𝑐. The first is a

fallback program 𝑓𝑑that satisfies (Φ𝑙, Ψ𝑙) and guards against edge cases where the GM hallucinates

#### or produces specification violating outputs. Without a verified fallback, the rejection sampler


#### could exhaust all samples; the fallback ensures that the contract holds unconditionally for all

parameter values. Although 𝑓𝑑and FGGM share the same Hoare triplet their output on individual

#### concrete points differ. Typically, 𝑓𝑑lacks the GM’s capability, resulting in lower reward, but it

prevents the agent from entering unsafe program states during execution by guaranteeing constraint
satisfaction. By combining the fallback 𝑓𝑑with the GM, FGGM retains the correctness guarantees
of 𝑓𝑑(Theorem 5.4) and the reward gains from the GM (Theorem 5.10). The second is a prompting

#### program 𝑓𝑝, which constructs the input to the GM. For LLMs, 𝑓𝑝encodes task-specific natural-


#### language instructions, analogous to prompt-tuning [6, 23], known to improve GM performance

and instruction adherence.
function 𝑖𝑑(𝑥1 : 𝑇1, . . . ,𝑥𝑛: 𝑇𝑛) : (𝑇𝑜)
requires Φ𝑙(𝑥1, . . . ,𝑥𝑛) {
var 𝑝: 𝑇𝑖𝑛,𝑦: 𝑇𝑜,𝑒𝑟𝑟: 𝑠𝑡𝑟:= “”;

4.2.3
Design Considerations. Alternative approaches for enforcing formal constraints on generative

#### model calls exist, but they fall short of FGGM. Constrained decoding [4, 37, 39, 50] modifies the


#### model’s internal decoding procedure, which limits its applicability to open-source models and

restricts constraints to syntactic forms such as context-free grammars. Post-hoc output filtering
without a fallback provides no guarantees: if all sampled outputs are rejected, the program produces
no valid output. Fully verifying an agentic program is computationally intractable, as it depends
on large parametric components such as LLMs. FGGM addresses all three limitations: it operates
solely on model outputs, making it compatible with closed-source models; it incorporates a verified

#### fallback that guarantees a valid output on every execution; and it decomposes verification into


#### parameter-independent local contracts. Moreover, these local contracts can be utilized during

learning to guide parameter tuning, improving conformance, and reducing reliance on the fallback.
4.2.4
Conformance Tuning. A FGGM that always uses the fallback satisfies (Φ𝑙, Ψ𝑙) but is practically
no better than a non-parametric program 𝑓𝑑; conformance tuning helps avoid this by teaching the

#### GM to satisfy the defined contract directly. The local contracts (Φ𝑙, Ψ𝑙) define the local learning

objective to avoid the fallback and assign higher probability to outputs that pass the check 𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘Ψ𝑙.
The objective in § 5.3.3 (Eq. 21) maximizes the expected contract satisfaction of GM samples over
an input set P. For any 𝜃∈Θ, the inner term R𝜃
Φ𝑙,Ψ𝑙(𝑝) measures whether a sampled output from
L𝜃(𝑝) satisfies 𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘Ψ𝑙(𝑥1, . . . ,𝑥𝑛,𝑦), while RΦ𝑙,Ψ𝑙(𝜃) aggregates this satisfaction across inputs in
P. By optimizing 𝜃∈Θ to increase this reward, the GM post-tuning is encouraged to concentrate

#### the probability mass on the support defined by (Φ𝑙, Ψ𝑙) (details in § 5.3.3). Overall conformance


#### tuning improves the acceptance rate of the rejection sampler, reducing reliance on 𝑓𝑑. Practical

impact of conformance tuning is shown in § 6.3 (Table 7) and § 6.4 (Table 8).

#### 4.2.5


#### Theoretical Evaluation. We show that the FGGM design does not reduce the theoretical

expressivity of transformer-based models (Theorem 5.11). Specifically, with an appropriate choice
of model parameters and a sufficiently large number of autoregressive decoding steps, commonly
referred to as Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning, the underlying generative model can simulate any
given Turing machine 𝑀[33]. Consequently, FGGM avoids the known expressivity loss associated
with naive constrained decoding methods [4]. Note that an appropriate output processor 𝑓𝑝𝑜𝑠𝑡can
remove intermediate CoT reasoning tokens and extract the final output from the raw GM output.

#### Further, based on the loss obtained from the unconstrained learning step, we compute a lower

bound on the cumulative reward of FGGM (Theorem 5.10).
4.3
SEVerA

#### In this section, we briefly discuss the purpose behind the four stages of SEVerA, the key design

decisions underlying each stage, and the theoretical properties of the overall framework.

#### 4.3.1


#### Ideation: The planner LLM defines task-specific generative components through FGGMs,

together with their prompting programs 𝑓𝑝, and expands the user-provided component set. This

#### is analogous to the abstraction phase of popular library learning approaches [10, 51], which


#### learn task-specific abstractions and extend the synthesis DSL. All proposed FGGM definitions

are subsequently verified for well-formedness. Specifically, the non-parametric fallback program

#### 𝑓𝑑is written in a verification-aware language such as Dafny, whose built-in verifier identifies


#### failing program paths and provides them as feedback to the planner LLM for refinement. Note

that direct calls to parametric GMs are disallowed; each must be wrapped within a verified FGGM.

#### 4.3.2


#### Component-Based Synthesis: In this step,

SEVerA synthesizes programs by combining com-

#### ponents from the expanded component set. It im-

plements a CEGIS-style loop, with the planner LLM
sampling candidate programs in Dafny (search) and

#### the built-in Dafny verifier acting as the checker,

providing unverified program paths as counterex-
amples to the planner LLM for subsequent refine-

#### ment. Provided that all FGGM components are

well-formed and the synthesized program verifies
against the user specification, compliance is guaran-
teed for all choices of generative model parameters.

#### 4.3.3


#### Alternative Synthesis Approach: Both 𝑓𝑑


#### synthesis and agent synthesis are instances of

component-based synthesis. Consequently, SEVerA
can leverage any existing component-based synthe-
sis algorithm [13, 14]. However, classical synthesiz-
ers typically make simplifying assumptions, such as
using type signatures as proxies for component con-
tracts and behavioral specifications. Moreover, they
have two key limitations: (i) they cannot leverage
natural-language task descriptions, which provide
valuable context for guiding the planner LLM, and
(ii) they rely heavily on testing to prune incorrect
candidates, which can incur substantial cost by ex-

#### ploring many irrelevant candidates, especially in

the presence of multiple GM calls. In contrast, the
planner LLM-based search–verify loop provides a simple and practically effective mechanism for
generating high-quality candidate programs, as demonstrated by our experiments.

#### 4.3.4


#### Parameter Learning: Since the compliance guarantees hold for all parameter values, the


#### learning step can apply unconstrained gradient-based optimization to tune the GM parameters.

Parameter tuning is guided by both the reward oracle R and the local conformance rewards from
each FGGM. We show that the parameters of each FGGM can be optimized independently, ensuring
scalability. For closed-source LLMs whose parameters are inaccessible, the learning step is skipped,
and improvements rely solely on prompt tuning through 𝑓𝑝.

#### 4.3.5


#### Candidate Pool and Execution Feedback. SEVerA maintains a pool of verified candidate


#### agentic programs and their fine-tuned parameters (Fig. 1). It greedily selects the candidate with

the highest cumulative reward R and executes it on D to identify soft counterexamples, i.e., inputs
whose reward falls below a user-defined threshold. The execution traces of these counterexamples
are then used as feedback for the planner LLM to refine the candidate programs. The number of

#### candidate sampling iterations is capped by the user. Once this limit is reached, SEVerA returns

the candidate in the pool with the highest cumulative reward R. Currently, SEVerA uses a simple
greedy selection strategy, which already performs well in practice (see § 6.2). More sophisticated
approaches, such as multi-armed bandits with Thompson sampling for candidate evaluation [46],
could further improve the exploration–exploitation trade-off.
, Vol. 1, No. 1, Article . Publication date: October 2026.

which can be formally enforced by static resource analysis (see § 6.7). The parametric candidate

4.3.7
Theoretical Evaluation of SEVerA. Alongside soundness, we establish the theoretical effec-
tiveness of SEVerA in balancing specification compliance and reward optimization (Theorem 5.9).
Assuming that the reward oracle R penalizes constraint-violating outputs and that there exists a
non-parametric fallback program 𝑓𝑑over F𝑐(recoverable by any classical synthesizer) satisfying

#### the global behavioral specification (Φ, Ψ), SEVerA can always recover a specification-compliant


#### program whose cumulative reward is at least that of the best available parametric model in F𝑝,


#### irrespective of the reward achieved by the fallback program. Furthermore, assuming parameter


#### optimization eventually recovers the ideal model parameters, SEVerA can recover the optimal

program by the FGGM expressivity result (Theorem 5.11).
5
SEVerA Details
5.1
Search Space
5.1.1
Program Search Space. We use a restricted subset of the popular verification-aware language
Dafny [28] to define the syntactically valid candidate programs. In this restricted subset, we allow
conditional blocks, while loops, assignment statements, assertion statements, function calls, and

#### annotations including invariant clauses and ranking functions (decreases clauses), along with


#### first-order input–output specifications. We present the BNF grammar 𝐺𝐷capturing this subset


#### in Appendix A. We allow four basic types T = {𝑏𝑜𝑜𝑙,𝑖𝑛𝑡,𝑟𝑒𝑎𝑙,𝑠𝑡𝑟} and list 𝑎𝑟𝑟⟨T⟩, basic built-in


#### arithmetic functions B𝑎= {+, −, ×, /}, boolean functions B𝑏= {&&, ||, !, =⇒}, and relations


#### B𝑟= {==, ≤, ≥, <, >} with their usual semantics. We use a planner LLM L𝑝: Σ∗→Σ∗to search

the program space by sampling candidate programs as strings. We assume that L𝑝’s output space
can represent all valid Dafny programs 𝐿(𝐺𝐷) ⊆Σ∗and that the type 𝑠𝑡𝑟can represent all strings

#### Σ∗over Σ. SEVerA provides built-in predicates 𝑙𝑒𝑥𝑇(𝑥: 𝑠𝑡𝑟) →𝑏𝑜𝑜𝑙to check whether a string


#### can be parsed as a type 𝑇∈{𝑏𝑜𝑜𝑙,𝑖𝑛𝑡,𝑟𝑒𝑎𝑙}. Additionally, SEVerA exposes a string processor


#### 𝑠𝑝𝑙𝑖𝑡: Σ × 𝑠𝑡𝑟→𝑎𝑟𝑟⟨𝑠𝑡𝑟⟩that splits a string on occurrences of a specified character 𝑐∈Σ,


#### enabling the definition of 𝑓𝑝𝑜𝑠𝑡in FGGM. Using Dafny as the language allows us to leverage its

built-in type-checker, verifier, and termination checker to check candidate programs against the
behavioral specification. While the framework is not language-specific, using a verification-aware
host language avoids re-implementing a custom verifier and termination checker.
, Vol. 1, No. 1, Article . Publication date: October 2026.

--- PAGE 13 ---

SEVerA : Verified Self-Evolving Agents with Specification Guidance
13
5.1.2
Library Components. The library F is partitioned into two disjoint sets, F = F𝑐∪F𝑝, where

#### F𝑐contains non-parametric components and F𝑝contains parametric components. Components


#### in F𝑐have fixed over-approximate semantics specified by contracts, while components in F𝑝

represent families of functions indexed by parameters. Each non-parametric component denotes

#### a pure, terminating function 𝑓𝑛: 𝑇1 × · · · × 𝑇𝑛→𝑇𝑜. For any inputs 𝑥𝑖∈𝑇𝑖, if 𝑓𝑛Φ(𝑥1, . . . ,𝑥𝑛)

holds and 𝑟= 𝑓𝑛(𝑥1, . . . ,𝑥𝑛), then 𝑓𝑛Ψ(𝑥1, . . . ,𝑥𝑛,𝑟) holds. Axiom set A contains the contracts of
all 𝑓𝑛∈F𝑐.

#### Definition 5.1 (Non-parametric component). 𝑓𝑛∈F𝑐of arity 𝑛is a tuple (𝑖𝑑(𝑓𝑛),𝜏𝑓𝑛, 𝑓𝑛Φ, 𝑓𝑛Ψ),

with identifier 𝑖𝑑(𝑓𝑛), type signature 𝜏𝑓𝑛= 𝑇1 × · · · ×𝑇𝑛→𝑇𝑜, and contract (𝑓𝑛Φ, 𝑓𝑛Ψ).

#### Definition 5.2 (Parametric component). 𝑓Θ

𝑛

#### ∈F𝑝of arity 𝑛is a tuple (𝑖𝑑(𝑓Θ

𝑛),𝜏𝑓Θ

#### 𝑛,𝜃0, Θ), with

identifier 𝑖𝑑(𝑓Θ
𝑛), type signature 𝜏𝑓Θ

A parametric component denotes the family of functions 𝑓Θ
𝑛= {𝑓𝜃
𝑛| 𝜃∈Θ}, where every instance

𝑛shares the same type signature 𝜏𝑓Θ

𝑛when the intended parameter value is clear
from context. The input to SEVerA is I = (F, A, R, D, Φ, Ψ, 𝐼), where F is the component library,
A contains the contracts for components in F𝑐, R is a pointwise black-box reward oracle, D is the
fixed training dataset, (Φ, Ψ) is the behavioral specification, and 𝐼is textual task information.
The initial synthesis context C = (𝐺𝐷, F𝑐, A) includes the grammar, non-parametric components,

#### and their contracts. The synthesis context grows during the ideation step with the well-formed


#### FGGM definitions. We use PC : Σ∗→{𝑇, 𝐹}, VC[Φ, Ψ] : Σ∗→{𝑇, 𝐹}, and TC : Σ∗→{𝑇, 𝐹} to


#### denote the parser, verifier, and termination checker, respectively. The verifier checks candidate

programs against (Φ, Ψ), while the termination checker proves termination. Both procedures run
under a fixed timeout and return false if they cannot prove the property within the allotted budget.

5.2.1
FGGM Formal Definition. Eq. 15 specifies the high-level syntax rules defining an FGGM. The
FGGM definition includes: (a) id: is the unique identifier of the created FGGM; (b) GMid: identifies the
underlying GM used as the proposal distribution; (c) typeSig: defines the type signature of the FGGM,
including input variables with their types and the output type, e.g., (𝑥1 : 𝑇1, . . . ,𝑥𝑛: 𝑇𝑛) →𝑇𝑜; (d)

#### (Φ𝑙, Ψ𝑙): are quantifier-free formulas whose terms can include non-parametric library functions

from F𝑐; (e) 𝑓𝑝: is the program that constructs the GM input from the typed input variables and the
task description; (f) 𝑓𝑑: is a non-parametric fallback program that satisfies the local specifications
(Φ𝑙, Ψ𝑙); and (g) 𝑓𝑝𝑜𝑠𝑡: processes the raw GM output and can optionally remove part of the response.

#### SEVerA parses these inputs, validates the definition, and automatically synthesizes a rejection

sampler with fallback, as shown in Fig. 2 where LΘ is the parametric GM specified by GMid. The
defined FGGM is then invoked in the candidate program via its identifier 𝑖𝑑. For valid definitions,

#### SEVerA synthesizes 𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘Ψ𝑙, which checks whether a concrete input–output pair (𝑥1, . . . ,𝑥𝑛,𝑦)

satisfies the proposed contract (Φ𝑙, Ψ𝑙), as elaborated in § 5.2.3.
We provide the BNF grammar for parsing the FGGM definition in Appendix N. Next, we discuss
correctness checks for a proposed FGGM definition. Provided SEVerA validates the definition includ-

#### ing the fallback 𝑓𝑑, ∀𝜃∈Θ.∀𝑥1 ∈𝑇1 . . . ∀𝑥𝑛∈𝑇𝑛.Φ𝑙(𝑥1, . . . ,𝑥𝑛) =⇒Ψ𝑙(𝑥1, . . . ,𝑥𝑛,𝑖𝑑(𝑥1, . . . ,𝑥𝑛))

holds ensuring formal contracts are preserved for all parametric values 𝜃∈Θ.

#### FGGM Well-formedness. Given an FGGM definition G = (𝑖𝑑G, 𝑓Θ

G ,𝜏G, Φ𝑙, Ψ𝑙, 𝑓𝑝, 𝑓𝑑, 𝑓𝑝𝑜𝑠𝑡)

#### following Eq. 15, this step checks whether the definition is well-formed. Let the parametric GM

identified by GMid be LΘ : 𝑇𝑖𝑛→𝑇𝑜. SEVerA first checks the well-formedness of the type signature
𝜏G = (𝑇1 × · · · ×𝑇𝑛) →𝑇𝑜and the well-formedness of the local contracts (Φ𝑙, Ψ𝑙). We then verify
that all terms in Φ𝑙and subsequently in Ψ𝑙that involve non-parametric functions from F𝑐are valid

#### (details in Appendix O). The G definition is considered valid provided both 𝑓𝑝, 𝑓𝑑and 𝑓𝑝𝑜𝑠𝑡are

type-checked and terminating, and the fallback 𝑓𝑑satisfies the contract VC[Φ𝑙, Ψ𝑙](𝑓𝑑). After the

#### validity check, SEVerA constructs 𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘Ψ𝑙: (𝑇1 × · · · × 𝑇𝑛× 𝑇𝑜) →{𝑇, 𝐹} that checks whether a


#### concrete input–output tuple (𝑥1, . . . ,𝑥𝑛,𝑦) satisfies the local output contract Ψ𝑙(see § 5.2.3). We

provide the pseudocode of 𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘Ψ𝑙in Appendix U and show it is sound and complete (Lemma 5.3).
Lemma 5.3 (Completeness of Checker). For any valid FGGM G with quantifier-free Ψ𝑙, ∀𝑥1 ∈
𝑇1, . . . , ∀𝑥𝑛∈𝑇𝑛, ∀𝑦∈𝑇𝑜. Φ𝑙(𝑥1, . . . ,𝑥𝑛) =⇒�𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘Ψ𝑙(𝑥1, . . . ,𝑥𝑛,𝑦) ⇐⇒Ψ𝑙(𝑥1, . . . ,𝑥𝑛,𝑦)�.

#### Proof Sketch. We prove this by structural induction on the quantifier-free formula Ψ𝑙. Base


#### case: If Ψ𝑙is an atomic predicate over terms built from computable library functions in F𝑐, then

under any concrete substitution (𝑥1, . . . ,𝑥𝑛,𝑦), 𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘Ψ𝑙evaluates the same computable terms and
returns the same boolean value as Ψ𝑙. Inductive step: Assume the claim holds for formulas 𝜙and

#### 𝜓. For compound formulas ¬𝜙, 𝜙∧𝜓, and 𝜙∨𝜓, 𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘Ψ𝑙recursively evaluates the subformulas

and applies the corresponding boolean operator, and returns the result. Thus, by induction on the
structure of Ψ𝑙, 𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘Ψ𝑙(𝑥1, . . . ,𝑥𝑛,𝑦) returns true iff Ψ𝑙(𝑥1, . . . ,𝑥𝑛,𝑦) holds whenever Φ𝑙(𝑥1, . . . ,𝑥𝑛)
holds. Hence, the checker is complete. Formal details in Appendix U.
□

and 𝑓𝑝𝑜𝑠𝑡are all terminating. In Eq. 16, we present a parameter-independent contract preservation

#### extends the initial synthesis context 𝐶with the

#### Definition 5.5 (Synthesizer). A component-

based synthesizer S takes as input a context 𝐶=

#### (𝐺𝐷, F, A) and a specification (Φ, Ψ). It either


#### synthesizes a program P{Θ} that satisfies (Φ, Ψ)

under 𝐶(see Eq. 8) or returns ⊥.

#### Any candidate program S(𝐶′, Φ𝑙, Ψ𝑙) = P{Θ}

may contain FGGM calls at distinct program locations. Each FGGM call introduces an optimizable
parameter, and {Θ} = {Θ1, . . . , Θ𝑘} denotes the set of all optimizable parameters in P{Θ}, where 𝑘
is the number of FGGM calls.

5.3.2
Synthesizer Implementation. In SEVerA, the component-based synthesizer uses planner-LLM-

#### guided search and verification, as shown in Algorithm 1. Given SEVerA’s input I and planner

L𝑝, the algorithm iteratively proposes candidate solutions within a fixed search budget 𝛿. At each

#### iteration, the planner synthesizes a set of FGGM definitions G (Line 6), which are checked for


#### well-formedness using 𝑣𝑎𝑙𝑖𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑒𝐹𝐺𝐺𝑀(Line 7). It also synthesizes an FGGM-specific prompting

function 𝑓𝑝. If the FGGM definitions are valid, the planner samples a candidate parametric program
P{Θ} that may invoke these FGGMs (Line 11), and the verification context is extended with their
contracts (Line 12). The sampled program is then checked for syntax, termination, and compliance

#### with the behavioral specification using 𝑣𝑎𝑙𝑖𝑑𝑎𝑡𝑒𝑃𝑟𝑜𝑔𝑟𝑎𝑚(Line 13). If verification succeeds, the


#### algorithm returns P{Θ} (Lines 14–15); otherwise, it continues using error feedback from failing

program paths until the search budget is exhausted (Lines 16–18).
5.3.3
Learner Implementation. The verification step guarantees that P{Θ} satisfies the behavioral
specification (Φ, Ψ) for all parameter values (Theorem 5.6). Thus, learning can optimize parameters
without violating (Φ, Ψ). However, solving the general objective in Eq. 20 over {Θ} with a black-box
reward R is practically intractable. Instead, SEVerA uses scalable gradient-based optimization to

#### For each local contract (Φ𝑙, Ψ𝑙), the conformance reward RΦ𝑙,Ψ𝑙encourages parameters to pro-


#### duce outputs that satisfy the contract. Improving conformance increases the success rate of the

corresponding rejection sampler, thereby reducing reliance on the static non-parametric fallback
program. Since the fallback cannot be tuned and typically has low reward (Table 8), reducing fall-
back usage helps optimize the final reward R more effectively. Our ablation study in § 6.3 supports

#### this observation. For a single FGGM with local contract (Φ𝑙, Ψ𝑙) and parameter 𝜃, Eq. 21 defines

the conformance reward over the set of GM inputs, or prompts, P𝐷collected from all executions

that satisfy 𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘Ψ𝑙, while RΦ𝑙,Ψ𝑙(𝜃) aggregates this satisfaction across inputs.

Jointly optimizing all parameters under Eq. 22 is feasible for smaller neural networks but becomes
expensive for agents with multiple interdependent LLM calls. To scale learning, we exploit a key

#### structural property: each conformance term R𝑖


#### Φ𝑙,Ψ𝑙(𝜃𝑖) depends only on the local inputs to the

corresponding FGGM and the outputs of its underlying GM, not on parameters of other FGGMs.
This independence holds because each FGGM’s rejection sampler and 𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘Ψ𝑙checker operate only
on its local contract (Φ𝑖

𝑙) and generated outputs. Therefore, the conformance term decomposes
across FGGM calls. For larger models, we approximate the augmented objective by optimizing each
FGGM 𝑖𝑑𝜃𝑖with parameter 𝜃𝑖independently, as shown in Eq. 23. The first term includes the task
reward R only when one of the GM’s outputs 𝑦𝑙∈{𝑦1, . . . ,𝑦𝐾} matches the agent’s final output 𝑦
on input 𝑥, where (𝑥, _) ∈D. This modular objective enables parallel training across different 𝜃𝑖.

Φ𝑙,Ψ𝑙(𝑝𝑥) + 𝜖)
(23)
For generative models with accessible parameters, the objective in Eq. 23 can be optimized using

#### reinforcement learning–based fine-tuning methods such as GRPO [40] (details in Appendix P).

Eq. 23 defines the reward for an input 𝑝𝑥, with the learning objective of maximizing the total reward

#### over all inputs 𝑝𝑥∈P. The reward function combines two signals: (1) the task-specific reward

R(𝑥, _,𝑦), active when the GM output 𝑦𝑙matches the final agent output 𝑦, and (2) the conformance

#### checker 𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘Ψ𝑙, which provides a bonus for outputs satisfying the local contract. The reward


#### function need not be differentiable w.r.t. 𝜃𝑖, as long as the outputs 𝑦𝑙remain differentiable w.r.t.

𝜃𝑖[40]. For closed-source models with inaccessible parameters, the learning step is skipped, and

#### performance improvements rely solely on prompt tuning through the synthesized 𝑓𝑝programs

within each FGGM.
5.3.4
SEVerA. Algorithm 2 summarizes the overall workflow of SEVerA. The algorithm maintains a

#### pool P of verified candidate agents and iteratively improves it through a search–verify–learn


#### loop. In each iteration, SEVerA first invokes the programSearch procedure (Algorithm 1) to

5.4.2
Reward Maximization. In this section, we theoretically evaluate SEVerA’s strength to maxi-
mize task reward R. First, we characterize a sufficient condition ensuring the existence of a candidate
𝑓∈S(𝐺, F) that satisfies (Φ, Ψ) and achieves no less reward than any generative model 𝑓𝜃
𝑛∈F𝑝
with any prompting function 𝑓𝑝∈S(𝐺, F𝑐) and any parameter 𝜃(Theorem 5.9). Secondly, we show

#### that the lower bound of the reward of 𝑓grows monotonically with the increase of the modular

reward R𝑎(𝜃𝑖) (Theorem 5.10). This shows improvement in R𝑎translates to improving task utility.
Finally, we show the proposed FGGM design retains the expressivity of transformer-based models
with sufficiently many reasoning steps. Hence, with ideal weights FGGM can simulate any given
Turing machine 𝑀with sufficiently many auto-regressive steps.
Definition 5.8 (Well-formed Reward). A reward function R is well-formed with respect to (Φ, Ψ) if

#### it assigns strictly higher reward to constraint-satisfying outputs than constraint-violating ones:

∀𝑥∈𝑇𝑖, ∀𝑦,𝑦′ ∈𝑇𝑜. (Φ(𝑥) ∧¬Ψ(𝑥,𝑦) ∧Ψ(𝑥,𝑦′)) =⇒R(𝑥, _,𝑦′) > R(𝑥, _,𝑦).

Finally, we show that the FGGM construction does not reduce the theoretical expressivity of the
underlying GM. Since the GM inside an FGGM generates without constraints, it can use arbitrarily
many autoregressive steps for intermediate reasoning before producing the final answer. By defining
𝑓𝑝𝑜𝑠𝑡with the built-in 𝑠𝑝𝑙𝑖𝑡function and an appropriate delimiter, the reasoning tokens are stripped
and only the final answer is checked against Ψ𝑙. This allows the GM to perform chain-of-thought
computation of arbitrary polynomial length while the local contract constrains only the extracted
output. Consequently, FGGM avoids the expressivity collapse to the DLOGTIME-uniform𝑇𝐶0 class
that arises when naive constrained decoding restricts every generated token [4].
Theorem 5.11 (FGGM Expressivity). For any Turing machine 𝑀with tape alphabet Γ and any
valid FGGM G with quantifier-free local contract (Φ𝑙, Ψ𝑙), if 𝑀computes outputs satisfying Ψ𝑙on all
inputs satisfying Φ𝑙, then there exists a constant-depth LLM L𝑀with log-precision, a delimiter 𝑐∉Γ,
and a post-processor 𝑓𝑝𝑜𝑠𝑡using 𝑠𝑝𝑙𝑖𝑡(𝑐, ·) such that for any input 𝑥with |𝑥| = 𝑛, the FGGM with GM
L𝑀returns 𝑀(𝑥) without triggering the fallback, assuming 𝑀halts on 𝑥within 𝑡(𝑛) steps where 𝑡(𝑛)
is polynomial in 𝑛.
Proof Sketch. By existing results on CoT expressivity [33], there exists a constant-depth LLM
that simulates 𝑀using 𝑡(𝑛) autoregressive steps, producing reasoning tokens followed by 𝑀(𝑥).

#### We insert a fresh delimiter 𝑐between the reasoning and the final answer, and define 𝑓𝑝𝑜𝑠𝑡to


#### extract the substring after 𝑐via 𝑠𝑝𝑙𝑖𝑡. The processed output 𝑀(𝑥) satisfies Ψ𝑙by assumption, so

by completeness of 𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘Ψ𝑙(Lemma 5.3), the rejection sampler accepts on the first sample. Formal
details in Appendix V.
□

SEVerA : Verified Self-Evolving Agents with Specification Guidance
19
6
Evaluation
We evaluate SEVerA on four tasks spanning scientific discovery, program verification, mathematical

#### reasoning, and agentic tool use. The evaluation seeks to answer five questions: whether formal

constraints improve safety (RQ1), whether learning remains effective under those constraints (RQ2),
how conformance tuning with local constraints improves performance (RQ3), how often the verified

#### fallback is triggered and whether the gains are attributable to search-space pruning rather than


#### the fallback (RQ4), and whether the stateful 𝐸𝑟𝑟Ψ𝑙feedback loop outperforms stateless rejection

sampling (RQ5). We also provide a qualitative analysis of the synthesized agents.
6.1
Experimental Setup
6.1.1
Tasks and Datasets. We instantiate SEVerA on four tasks, each representative of a different
kind of constrained agent synthesis problem.
LLM-Assisted Program Verification (DafnyBench). The agent receives a Dafny program with
pre-encoded input-output specifications and must synthesize annotations that enable verification
against the pre-encoded specification inside the input program [32]. The behavioral specification

#### of the agent requires the output to remain parsable and equivalent to the input modulo added

annotations. The task performance is measured by verification success within a fixed time budget.

#### The underlying LLM is Claude Sonnet 4.5 [2], a closed-source model whose weights are not

accessible; consequently, the learning step (i.e., parameter tuning) is not applicable to this task.
Symbolic Math Synthesis (GSM-Symbolic). GSM-Symbolic is a benchmark of grade-school math

#### word problems generated from symbolic templates [34]. We use it to evaluate the LLM’s ability


#### to synthesize correct symbolic mathematical expressions. Behavioral specification of the agent

enforces that each generated expression is syntactically valid with respect to a formal grammar,
and task performance measures whether the answer is equivalent to the ground-truth expression.

#### Agentic Tool Use (𝜏2-bench). 𝜏2-bench [5] evaluates conversational LLM agents in realistic

customer-service scenarios. We use the retail and airline domains. The agent must select and invoke
API tools to resolve user requests while respecting domain-specific policies. Unlike the other tasks,

#### the hard constraint and the utility metric operate at different granularities. The hard constraint


#### is enforced at each individual tool call: before every tool call is executed, the Agent-C checker


#### [22] verifies that the proposed call complies with temporal policy-compliance rules (e.g., refund

eligibility, booking-modification policies) given the trace of prior calls. Task performance (pass rate)
is measured over the entire multi-turn interaction trace as the percentage of interactions where the
agent successfully resolves the user request.
Constrained Symbolic Regression (SymReg). In symbolic regression, each synthesis instance

#### consists of noisy input–output observations generated by an unknown ground-truth function


#### 𝑓𝑔𝑡, along with a behavioral specification encoding prior knowledge about 𝑓𝑔𝑡. The goal is to


#### synthesize a neuro-symbolic program (i.e., the agent) that fits the observed input–output pairs

while provably satisfying the behavioral specifications. We consider a total of 35 synthesis tasks,
each with 2 different training sets corresponding to varying noise levels (5%, 10%), and behavioral
specifications that encode symbolic bounds on the ground truth. Each training set includes 600 noisy
samples and 400 test samples. All synthesis instances are drawn from popular benchmarks [7, 19].

#### 6.1.2


#### Implementation Details. All experiments are conducted on NVIDIA A100 GPUs (40 GB).

Across all tasks we use the same high-level pipeline: planner-guided program search with a budget
of Δ = 10 iterations, FGGM-based rejection sampling with a fixed sample budget 𝐾= 5, deductive

#### verification with bounded verifier and SMT timeouts, and hyperparameter tuning when model

parameters are accessible. The planner LLM is provided with 3 ground-truth examples as in-context
examples (2 for 𝜏2-bench, due to the long traces); these examples are excluded from all train and
, Vol. 1, No. 1, Article . Publication date: October 2026.

--- PAGE 20 ---

20
Banerjee et al.
test splits. The full planner prompt template is given in Appendix T, and details on how execution
feedback is constructed and provided to the planner between iterations are given in Appendix S.
6.1.3
Learning Implementation. Once a program verifies, SEVerA uses Dafny’s built-in transpiler
[9] to translate the verified code into Python. Parameter tuning and experiments are then conducted
on the transpiled code. SEVerA assumes that Dafny’s transpiler is sound for this restricted subset
of the language.

#### 6.1.4


#### Metrics. For each task, we report: (a) the constraint violation rate on a held-out test set of

unseen inputs, measuring the fraction of outputs that violate the behavioral specification (Φ, Ψ);

#### (b) task performance, measured by mean squared error for SymReg, verification success rate for

Dafny, answer accuracy for GSM-Symbolic, and pass rate for 𝜏2-bench; and (c) wall-clock time.
6.1.5
Baseline. For all four tasks outside GSM-Symbolic, we consider state-of-the-art (SOTA) hand-
crafted agents. For GSM-Symbolic, we use the SOTA constrained decoder [4]. Some hand-crafted
agents (e.g., Agent-C on 𝜏2-bench) manually enforce hard constraints; however, being static, they
achieve lower task performance. We also consider self-evolving frameworks without constraints,
which do not verify the generated programs against behavioral specifications and accept all planner
programs provided they are syntactically valid and type-checked. Consequently, such frameworks
provide no guarantees about the correctness or safety of the generated code. To ensure fairness,
unconstrained self-evolving frameworks are only allowed to use the same set of library functions.

#### 6.1.6


#### Verification Success. For program verification, tool calling, and GSM-Symbolic, SEVerA

consistently finds a valid program satisfying (Φ, Ψ) within 10 attempts. For symbolic regression, it
produces a verified program in 33 out of 35 cases, whereas the baselines generate at least 11 cases
where the generated program violates the behavioral specification on test inputs.

Dataset splits. Table 1 summarizes the dataset

#### sizes for each synthesis task. For each task, a small

#### 6.1.8


#### Task-specific details. For Dafny, the underlying LLM is Claude Sonnet 4.5 [2], a closed-


#### source model; because model weights are inaccessible, Dafny experiments use only the search

and verification stages of SEVerA and do not perform parameter tuning. For 𝜏2-bench, parameter
tuning is omitted because the long, multi-turn execution traces make gradient-based fine-tuning
prohibitively expensive. For GSM-Symbolic, we use GRPO [40] with LoRA adapters [20] on Qwen3-
8B [47]. Additional hyperparameters (LoRA rank, solver timeouts, etc.) are reported in Appendix Q.
6.2
Main Results
We begin with the central question motivating SEVerA:
RQ1 (Safety). What goes wrong without formal constraints, and does SEVerA eliminate these failures?
We compare unconstrained agent synthesis against SEVerA across all four tasks to show that hard
behavioral specifications are necessary: without them, agents silently produce invalid outputs on
unseen inputs that are undetectable by soft performance metrics.

6.2.1
LLM-Assisted Program Verification (Dafny). Table 2 presents results on two Dafny program-

#### verification benchmarks and offers the clearest illustration of why formal constraints are indis-


#### pensable. The gap between the Verif. and Verif. & NoDiff columns reveals the core safety issue


#### (RQ1): without hard constraints, agents inflate their verification rate by modifying the original


#### program. The LLM baseline reports 76.8% verification on HumanEvalDafny, yet only 73.7% of

those outputs actually preserve the input program. Overall, 8.1% of all outputs are violations that

#### would go undetected by a metric that checks verification alone. The DafnyBench baseline and


#### SEVerA without constraints reduce but do not eliminate this problem (4.0% and 5.1% violations,

respectively). Only the full SEVerA pipeline, which enforces the NoDiff behavioral specification,
achieves a 0% violation rate on both benchmarks while simultaneously attaining the highest Verif.
& NoDiff rate (97.0% on HumanEvalDafny, 89.1% on DafnyBench). This confirms that FGGM-based

#### rejection sampling with a verified fallback does not merely filter outputs but actively steers the

planner toward higher-quality candidates. This result is particularly notable because it is achieved

#### without any parameter tuning (the underlying model, Claude Sonnet 4.5, is closed-source), the

gains come entirely from making constraints visible to the planner and enforcing them at synthesis
time. The overhead introduced by SEVerA is modest. The full pipeline takes 18.2 s per instance on
HumanEvalDafny compared to 9.8 s for LLM generation, roughly a 1.9× factor that includes Dafny
verification and up to 𝑘LLM calls. On DafnyBench the factor is 2.5× (25.6 s vs. 10.3 s). Given that
the LLM baseline produces untrustworthy outputs on 8 to 10% of inputs, we believe this overhead
is justified for the formal guarantees it provides.

#### 6.2.2


#### Agentic Tool Use (𝜏2-bench). Table 3 reports performance on 𝜏2-bench. The LLM baseline

(Qwen3-8B without constraints) violates domain policies on 76.3% of retail interactions and 68.4%
of airline interactions, yielding pass rates of only 11.3% and 13.2%, respectively. Both Agent-C and

#### SEVerA reduce the violation rate to 0%, confirming that formal policy enforcement is necessary.

SEVerA without constraints illustrates an intermediate point: by running the search loop without

#### informing the planner of the policy specification, SEVerA (w/o constraints) improves pass rates


#### to 49.4% (retail) and 44.7% (airline), but still incurs 10.3% and 25.5% violation rates, respectively.

Enforcing constraints eliminates all violations while further improving pass rates to 53.6% and 52.6%.

#### This gap reflects SEVerA’s search-verify-learn design. By synthesizing agent programs that are

verified to satisfy the per-call policy specification, SEVerA can explore a wider space of compliant
strategies rather than relying solely on runtime constraint checking. The per-call hard constraint

#### ensures that every individual tool call is policy-compliant, while the search loop optimizes the

agent’s overall strategy to maximize the end-of-trace pass rate. Impressively, SEVerA with small
Qwen3-8B outperforms Agent-C with Claude Sonnet 4.5 (52.6 vs 47.3) (result from [22]).
SEVerA is also faster than Agent-C in both domains (212.4s vs. 234.7s in retail and 241.1s vs. 272.6s
in airline). The LLM baseline is fastest because it performs no policy checking, but its outputs are
unusable in practice given the violation rates.
6.2.3
Symbolic Math Synthesis (GSM-Symbolic). GSM-Symbolic uses Qwen3-8B as the underlying
LLM. Qwen3-8B is open-weight, making parameter tuning feasible. This allows us to ask:
RQ2 (Training Impact). Does parameter tuning improve task performance under formal constraints?
Table 4 compares SEVerA against the LLM baseline and CRANE [4], a state-of-the-art constrained-

#### decoding method, on GSM-Symbolic using Qwen3-8B. The LLM baseline (Qwen3-8B) achieves

only 38.3% accuracy with a 10.6% constraint violation rate (RQ1), confirming that a vanilla LLM fre-
quently produces outputs that are syntactically or semantically invalid. CRANE improves accuracy
to 44.7% and reduces violations to 2.1% by augmenting the output grammar with reasoning tokens.
1SEVerA without parameter tuning already achieves 53.2% accuracy and eliminates all violations,

#### an 8.5% accuracy improvement over CRANE with strictly stronger correctness guarantees. This


#### improvement stems from the FGGM mechanism: the rejection sampler with a verified fallback

ensures that every output satisfies the formal grammar and the semantic specification, while the
synthesized prompting program 𝑓𝑝guides the underlying LLM toward correct answers.
We also compare the performance of SEVerA with and without parameter tuning (RQ2). We fine-
tune Qwen3-8B via GRPO with LoRA adapters under the same formal constraints. This provides a
further 12.8% accuracy gain (from 53.2% to 66.0%) while maintaining the zero-violation guarantee.

#### The tuned model produces outputs that more often pass the FGGM checker 𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘Ψ𝑙on the first

sample, reducing reliance on the fallback and thereby improving answer quality. Notably, the tuned
1CRANE only ensures that at every decoding step, the generated partial output is a valid prefix of the target grammar.

6.2.4
Constrained Symbolic Regression. In constrained symbolic regression, we consider the SOTA
genetic programming–based method PySR [8], as well as the existing self-evolving method LLM-SR
[41], which samples parametric programs with optimizable PyTorch parameters and refines them to
minimize loss. We denote the noise level by𝜖. As shown in Table 5, both baselines frequently produce
programs that violate the behavioral specifications on the test data, with violations occurring in
up to 62.86% of synthesis instances for PySR and up to 34.29% for LLM-SR. For both noise levels,
SEVerA finds a verified solution in 33 out of 35 instances. Table 6 presents a pairwise comparison
between SEVerA and the baselines in terms of normalized mean squared error (NMSE) on the test
dataset. We compute the average NMSE over only those instances where the baseline-generated
programs satisfy the behavioral constraints, pruning instances that correspond to invalid solutions.
SEVerA achieves significantly lower NMSE values compared to both baselines.
6.3
Constraint Decomposition

#### The previous section showed that formal constraints improve both safety and performance. We

now ask a finer-grained question:
RQ3 (Constraint Decomposition). What are the individual contributions of parameter tuning with local
FGGM contracts versus global reward R?
We ablate each component independently on GSM-Symbolic. Table 7 decomposes the 12.8% gain that
parameter tuning provides (from 53.2% to 66.0%, as shown in Table 4) into its two sources. Starting

#### from SEVerA without parameter tuning (53.2%), adding local-only tuning, which optimizes the

FGGM conformance reward so that the model’s outputs more frequently satisfy per-call contracts,
yields a modest improvement to 55.3%. This 2.1% gain indicates that parameter tuning alone helps
the model produce syntactically valid outputs more reliably, reducing reliance on rejection sampling.
Global-only tuning, which optimizes the task reward R without the FGGM conformance component,
provides a substantially larger gain to 61.7%. This 8.5% improvement reflects the direct benefit of
training the model to produce semantically correct answers. The full SEVerA pipeline combines both
and achieves 66.0%, a 4.3% gain. This demonstrates that the two training signals are complementary:
, Vol. 1, No. 1, Article . Publication date: October 2026.

--- PAGE 24 ---

24
Banerjee et al.
global tuning improves answer correctness while local conformance tuning ensures outputs satisfy
per-call contracts, jointly yielding the best overall accuracy.

6.6
Qualitative Analysis of Synthesized Programs

#### We examine what SEVerA synthesizes (full programs in Appendices K, L, and M). Exposing


#### the behavioral specification during evolution lets SEVerA identify each task’s specific failure


#### modes and synthesize FGGM components targeted at them, rather than a single generic agent.


#### On Dafny it builds a separate component per failure cause (𝑑𝑖𝑓𝑓𝐸𝑟𝑟𝑜𝑟𝐹𝐺𝐺𝑀for base-program

edits, 𝑣𝑒𝑟𝑖𝑓𝑖𝑒𝑟𝐸𝑟𝑟𝑜𝑟𝐹𝐺𝐺𝑀for genuine proof failures, and 𝑖𝑛𝑖𝑡𝑖𝑎𝑙𝐹𝐺𝐺𝑀for lemma-, generic-, or

#### set-specific guidance); on GSM-Symbolic, an escalation ladder of three progressively narrower

parse-repair FGGMs (𝑝𝑎𝑟𝑠𝑒𝐸𝑟𝑟𝑜𝑟𝐹𝐺𝐺𝑀1–𝑝𝑎𝑟𝑠𝑒𝐸𝑟𝑟𝑜𝑟𝐹𝐺𝐺𝑀3); and on𝜏2-bench, a duplicate-action
detector (𝑑𝑢𝑝𝑙𝑖𝑐𝑎𝑡𝑒𝐹𝐺𝐺𝑀) plus a policy-recovery component (𝑎𝑔𝑒𝑛𝑡𝐶𝐸𝑟𝑟𝑜𝑟𝐹𝐺𝐺𝑀) that inspects
the offending tool to supply missing prerequisites. In each case the local contract Ψ𝑙turns a failure
mode into a synthesis objective, so SEVerA specializes to that failure instead of collapsing into a
degenerate shortcut.
, Vol. 1, No. 1, Article . Publication date: October 2026.

SEVerA : Verified Self-Evolving Agents with Specification Guidance
25
This error-targeted synthesis is stronger than post-hoc filtering of a fixed agent’s outputs, which
is what the fallback baseline measures and is near-useless on its own (Table 8, FB Acc. 0.0–5.4%).

#### Even after excluding every fallback-triggered instance, the gain over the best baseline remains

large (+8.1 on HumanEvalDafny, +10.6 on 𝜏2-Retail, +21.3 on GSM-Symbolic), so the improvement
comes from the specialized program structure rather than from discarding bad outputs. The stateful
𝐸𝑟𝑟Ψ𝑙feedback (Section 6.5) reinforces this by folding the failing atomic formulas of Ψ𝑙back into
𝑓𝑝, so successive samples avoid repeating the identified error
6.7
Discussion

#### SEVerA’s runtime overhead is competitive: on 𝜏2-bench it is faster than Agent-C, on Dafny the

full pipeline incurs only a modest 1.9–2.5× slowdown relative to the LLM baseline, and on GSM-
Symbolic just ∼1.1×, primarily due to the verification and rejection-sampling steps. One current
limitation is that the formulation is resource-unaware. While the behavioral specifications constrain
functional correctness, they do not account for computational resources such as LLM calls, token
usage, or wall-clock budget. Extending SEVerA with resource-aware specifications [25], for example
by encoding token or call budgets as additional hard constraints within the FGGM framework, is a
promising direction for future work. Another direction is to explore alternative FGGM designs. For
example, instead of invoking a fallback when rejection sampling fails, an FGGM component could
raise an error that is then handled gracefully by the surrounding program. Conformance tuning
would then aim to minimize the frequency of such error cases.
7
Related Work

#### Self-Evolving Agents and Their Risks. Recent work has explored automatic agent synthesis

and self-improvement, from code-based action unification [54] and open-ended skill libraries [52]

#### to architecture search [15, 21, 58], gradient-free symbolic updates [56], co-evolution with world

models [11], and reinforcement-learning-driven skill refinement [48, 53]. However, none of these
provide formal behavioral guarantees, a gap made urgent by demonstrated risks of coding-agent
exploits [16, 17, 27], test-case exploitation [55], and verification cheating [3]. SEVerA addresses this
by imposing and verifying hard formal specifications before deployment.
Runtime Monitoring and Shielding. Rather than verifying at synthesis time, runtime approaches
enforce safety on observed executions. Shield synthesis from neural policies [57] and temporal-logic

#### runtime checking of tool-call sequences [22] are representative examples, but both are limited


#### to observed traces and cannot guarantee safety on unseen inputs. SEVerA instead verifies the

synthesized program for all inputs and parameter values at synthesis time. The monitoring checks
can be used to define the specification as done with [22].
Neuro-Symbolic Program Synthesis. Neuro-symbolic methods, including neural program gen-

#### eration from examples [38], type-directed differentiable programming [51], wake-sleep library

learning [10], vision-language program composition [45], and LLM-guided symbolic regression [41],
synthesize programs that invoke neural components but lack formal correctness guarantees over
the composed system. SEVerA extends this paradigm with FGGMs that bind each model call to a
verified local contract, enabling end-to-end verification.
Constrained Decoding. Constrained decoding restricts LLM outputs to a formal language through

#### grammar-based [39, 49, 50], diffusion-based [44], programming-abstraction [6, 23], reasoning-


#### preserving [4], type-based [36], and semantic [37] techniques. These approaches require access


#### to the model’s decoding internals (precluding closed-source models) and enforce per-token or

per-output constraints, not program-level behavioral specifications. SEVerA’s FGGM mechanism
operates on model outputs via rejection sampling and is model-agnostic.
, Vol. 1, No. 1, Article . Publication date: October 2026.

--- PAGE 26 ---

26
Banerjee et al.
Deductive Program Synthesis. Classical deductive synthesis, including syntax-guided [1], semantics-
guided [24], counterexample-guided [42], abstraction-guided [18], and component-based [12–14]
approaches, provides correctness guarantees but targets non-parametric programs and does not
consider task-specific rewards.
8
Conclusion
We presented SEVerA, a framework for specification-driven synthesis of self-evolving LLM agents
with formal behavioral guarantees. Its core abstraction, the Formally Guarded Generative Model

#### (FGGM), binds each generative-model call to a Hoare-style local contract enforced by a stateful

rejection sampler with a verified fallback. Because the fallback discharges the contract for every

#### parameter value, correctness is parameter-independent and survives arbitrary fine-tuning. This

lets SEVerA split the constrained learning problem into four stages (ideation, search, verification,
and learning), so that scalable methods such as GRPO can optimize task reward without putting
the specification at risk. We proved that SEVerA is sound, gave a sufficient condition under which

#### a verified agent matches or exceeds the reward of the best unconstrained model, and showed


#### that FGGMs retain the expressivity of the underlying transformer. Across program verification,

symbolic math synthesis, policy-compliant tool use, and constrained symbolic regression, SEVerA
attains zero constraint violations on held-out inputs while improving task performance over both
unconstrained frameworks and task-specific state of the art. These results suggest that behavioral
specifications play a dual role: certifying compliance on all inputs and, by pruning shortcut-laden
candidates, steering evolution toward higher-utility agents.
, Vol. 1, No. 1, Article . Publication date: October 2026.

::=

::=
⟨𝑖𝑑𝑒𝑛𝑡⟩: ⟨𝑡𝑦𝑝𝑒⟩
⟨𝑡𝑦𝑝𝑒⟩
::=
int | bool | string | real | arr⟨⟨𝑡𝑦𝑝𝑒⟩⟩
⟨𝑠𝑝𝑒𝑐𝑠⟩
::=
𝜖| ⟨𝑠𝑝𝑒𝑐⟩⟨𝑠𝑝𝑒𝑐𝑠⟩
⟨𝑠𝑝𝑒𝑐⟩
::=
requires ⟨𝑓𝑜𝑟𝑚𝑢𝑙𝑎⟩| ensures ⟨𝑓𝑜𝑟𝑚𝑢𝑙𝑎⟩
⟨𝑠𝑡𝑚𝑡_𝑙𝑖𝑠𝑡⟩
::=
𝜖| ⟨𝑠𝑡𝑚𝑡⟩⟨𝑠𝑡𝑚𝑡_𝑙𝑖𝑠𝑡⟩
⟨𝑠𝑡𝑚𝑡⟩
::=
⟨𝑎𝑠𝑠𝑖𝑔𝑛_𝑠𝑡𝑚𝑡⟩| ⟨𝑖𝑓_𝑠𝑡𝑚𝑡⟩| ⟨𝑤ℎ𝑖𝑙𝑒_𝑠𝑡𝑚𝑡⟩| ⟨𝑎𝑠𝑠𝑒𝑟𝑡_𝑠𝑡𝑚𝑡⟩| ⟨𝑐𝑎𝑙𝑙_𝑠𝑡𝑚𝑡⟩
⟨𝑎𝑠𝑠𝑖𝑔𝑛_𝑠𝑡𝑚𝑡⟩
::=
⟨𝑖𝑑𝑒𝑛𝑡⟩:= ⟨𝑒𝑥𝑝𝑟⟩;
⟨𝑐𝑎𝑙𝑙_𝑠𝑡𝑚𝑡⟩
::=
⟨𝑖𝑑𝑒𝑛𝑡⟩(⟨𝑎𝑟𝑔𝑠⟩);
⟨𝑎𝑟𝑔𝑠⟩
::=
𝜖| ⟨𝑒𝑥𝑝𝑟⟩| ⟨𝑒𝑥𝑝𝑟⟩, ⟨𝑎𝑟𝑔𝑠⟩
⟨𝑖𝑓_𝑠𝑡𝑚𝑡⟩
::=
if (⟨𝑓𝑜𝑟𝑚𝑢𝑙𝑎⟩){⟨𝑠𝑡𝑚𝑡_𝑙𝑖𝑠𝑡⟩}
| if (⟨𝑓𝑜𝑟𝑚𝑢𝑙𝑎⟩){⟨𝑠𝑡𝑚𝑡_𝑙𝑖𝑠𝑡⟩} else {⟨𝑠𝑡𝑚𝑡_𝑙𝑖𝑠𝑡⟩}
⟨𝑤ℎ𝑖𝑙𝑒_𝑠𝑡𝑚𝑡⟩
::=
while (⟨𝑓𝑜𝑟𝑚𝑢𝑙𝑎⟩) ⟨𝑙𝑜𝑜𝑝_𝑎𝑛𝑛𝑜𝑡𝑠⟩{⟨𝑠𝑡𝑚𝑡_𝑙𝑖𝑠𝑡⟩}
⟨𝑙𝑜𝑜𝑝_𝑎𝑛𝑛𝑜𝑡𝑠⟩
::=
𝜖| ⟨𝑙𝑜𝑜𝑝_𝑎𝑛𝑛𝑜𝑡⟩⟨𝑙𝑜𝑜𝑝_𝑎𝑛𝑛𝑜𝑡𝑠⟩
⟨𝑙𝑜𝑜𝑝_𝑎𝑛𝑛𝑜𝑡⟩
::=
invariant ⟨𝑓𝑜𝑟𝑚𝑢𝑙𝑎⟩| decreases ⟨𝑒𝑥𝑝𝑟⟩
⟨𝑎𝑠𝑠𝑒𝑟𝑡_𝑠𝑡𝑚𝑡⟩
::=
assert ⟨𝑓𝑜𝑟𝑚𝑢𝑙𝑎⟩;
⟨𝑒𝑥𝑝𝑟⟩
::=
⟨𝑎𝑒𝑥𝑝𝑟⟩| ⟨𝑠𝑒𝑥𝑝𝑟⟩| ⟨𝑎𝑟𝑟𝑒𝑥𝑝𝑟⟩
⟨𝑎𝑒𝑥𝑝𝑟⟩
::=

⟨𝑎𝑟𝑟𝑒𝑥𝑝𝑟⟩
::=

⟨𝑎𝑟𝑟𝑎𝑦_𝑙𝑖𝑡⟩
::=

SEVerA : Verified Self-Evolving Agents with Specification Guidance
31
B
Rejection Sampling Details

#### Rejection Sampling Details. Given a target distribution 𝜋𝑡and a proposal distribution 𝜋𝑝with

𝑆(𝜋𝑡) ⊆𝑆(𝜋𝑝), assume there exists 𝑀≥1 such that ∀𝑧∈Ω,
𝐷𝜋𝑡(𝑧) ≤𝑀· 𝐷𝜋𝑝(𝑧). The procedure

#### samples 𝑧∼𝜋𝑝and 𝑢∼Uniform(0, 1), and accepts 𝑧iff 𝑢≤

𝐷𝜋𝑡(𝑧)

#### 𝑀·𝐷𝜋𝑝(𝑧) . Otherwise, the sample is

rejected and the process repeats. Accepted samples follow 𝜋𝑡. Moreover, any 𝑧∉𝑆(𝜋𝑡) is always
rejected since 𝐷𝜋𝑡(𝑧) = 0, ensuring all accepted samples lie in 𝑆(𝜋𝑡). The acceptance probability is
1/𝑀, so efficiency depends on how tightly 𝜋𝑝upper-bounds 𝜋𝑡. For this paper, we assume 𝑀= 1.
C
Library Functions for Symbolic Regression
Library Function Set F.
• 𝑎𝑏𝑠(𝑥: 𝑟𝑒𝑎𝑙) →𝑟: 𝑟𝑒𝑎𝑙ensures 𝑟≥0 ensures 𝑟= 𝑥∨𝑟= −𝑥

K
Example Agents for LLM Assisted Program Verification

#### The following is an example verified agent program for the Dafny program verification task.

The agent defines and invokes three FGGMs, 𝑖𝑛𝑖𝑡𝑖𝑎𝑙𝐹𝐺𝐺𝑀(Fig. 4), 𝑑𝑖𝑓𝑓𝐸𝑟𝑟𝑜𝑟𝐹𝐺𝐺𝑀(Fig. 5), and
𝑣𝑒𝑟𝑖𝑓𝑖𝑒𝑟𝐸𝑟𝑟𝑜𝑟𝐹𝐺𝐺𝑀(Fig. 6), each with local contract Ψ𝑙: 𝑛𝑜𝐷𝑖𝑓𝑓(𝑏𝑎𝑠𝑒_𝑝𝑟𝑜𝑔𝑟𝑎𝑚, ·) and a fallback
that returns the original program (justified by the reflexivity axiom). All three share𝐺𝑀𝑖𝑑:= 𝑐𝑙𝑎𝑢𝑑𝑒

#### and differ only in the prompting function 𝑓𝑝and output processor 𝑓𝑝𝑜𝑠𝑡: the first generates an


#### initial annotation with context-specific guidance, the second repairs after a diff-checker failure,

and the third repairs after a verification failure with error-specific feedback. In each FGGM, 𝑓𝑝𝑜𝑠𝑡
extracts the annotated program from the fenced code block of the raw model response, while the
auto-synthesized error function 𝐸𝑟𝑟Ψ𝑙(which reports the failing atomic formulas of Ψ𝑙) is threaded
back to the prompt through its 𝑒𝑟𝑟argument, making the rejection sampler (Fig. 2) stateful across
iterations. The agent program (Fig. 7) dispatches to the appropriate FGGM on each loop iteration.

#### On HumanEvalDafny, the fallback fires on only 5.1% of instances (Table 8), confirming that the

rejection sampler with 𝐸𝑟𝑟Ψ𝑙feedback rarely exhausts all 𝐾samples.
, Vol. 1, No. 1, Article . Publication date: October 2026.

Φ𝑙:= true
Ψ𝑙:= 𝑛𝑜𝐷𝑖𝑓𝑓(𝑏𝑎𝑠𝑒_𝑝𝑟𝑜𝑔𝑟𝑎𝑚, 𝑓(𝑏𝑎𝑠𝑒_𝑝𝑟𝑜𝑔𝑟𝑎𝑚))
𝑓𝑝:= function 𝑓𝑝(𝑏𝑎𝑠𝑒_𝑝𝑟𝑜𝑔𝑟𝑎𝑚: 𝑠𝑡𝑟, 𝑒𝑟𝑟: 𝑠𝑡𝑟) : (𝑠𝑡𝑟) {
var 𝑔: 𝑠𝑡𝑟:=
“You are an expert in Dafny verification. Given the following unannotated Dafny code, ”
+ “add appropriate loop invariants, assertions, and decreases clauses to make it verify.\n\n”
+ “IMPORTANT RULES:\n”
+ “1. Do NOT modify the method signatures, requires clauses, or ensures clauses\n”
+ “2. Only add invariants, assertions, and decreases clauses\n”
+ “3. Do NOT change the logic or control flow\n”
+ “4. Keep invariants MINIMAL - only what’s necessary for postconditions\n”
+ “5. Add decreases clauses for termination\n”;
if (𝑖𝑠𝑆𝑢𝑏𝑠𝑡𝑟𝑖𝑛𝑔(“lemma ”, 𝑏𝑎𝑠𝑒_𝑝𝑟𝑜𝑔𝑟𝑎𝑚)) {
𝑔:= 𝑔
+ “6. LEMMAS: If a lemma has a non-trivial postcondition, add explicit proof steps:\n”
+ “
- Use assertions to unfold function definitions\n”
+ “
- Add case analysis or pattern matching if needed\n”
+ “
- Call other lemmas to establish intermediate facts\n”
+ “
- Do NOT leave lemma bodies empty unless postcondition is trivial\n”; }
if (𝑖𝑠𝑆𝑢𝑏𝑠𝑡𝑟𝑖𝑛𝑔(“<”, 𝑏𝑎𝑠𝑒_𝑝𝑟𝑜𝑔𝑟𝑎𝑚) ∧𝑖𝑠𝑆𝑢𝑏𝑠𝑡𝑟𝑖𝑛𝑔(“>”, 𝑏𝑎𝑠𝑒_𝑝𝑟𝑜𝑔𝑟𝑎𝑚)) {
𝑔:= 𝑔
+ “7. GENERICS: When calling generic functions/lemmas, propagate type constraints:\n”
+ “
- If a called function requires T(00), declare it in the caller too\n”
+ “
- Explicitly instantiate type parameters when needed\n”; }
if (𝑖𝑠𝑆𝑢𝑏𝑠𝑡𝑟𝑖𝑛𝑔(“set<”, 𝑏𝑎𝑠𝑒_𝑝𝑟𝑜𝑔𝑟𝑎𝑚) ∨𝑖𝑠𝑆𝑢𝑏𝑠𝑡𝑟𝑖𝑛𝑔(“set ”, 𝑏𝑎𝑠𝑒_𝑝𝑟𝑜𝑔𝑟𝑎𝑚)) {
𝑔:= 𝑔
+ “8. SETS: Use simple, direct assertions for set reasoning:\n”
+ “
