+ “
- Use cardinality constraints: |A| == |B| + |C|\n”
+ “
- Avoid complex witness variables or case analysis\n”
+ “
- Let Dafny’s built-in set theory automation work\n”; }
if (𝑖𝑠𝑆𝑢𝑏𝑠𝑡𝑟𝑖𝑛𝑔(“multiset”, 𝑏𝑎𝑠𝑒_𝑝𝑟𝑜𝑔𝑟𝑎𝑚)) {
𝑔:= 𝑔
+ “9. MULTISETS: Match the structure of postconditions directly\n”
+ “

+ “
- Avoid unnecessary intermediate steps\n”; }
return 𝑔+ “\nBase program:\n” + 𝑏𝑎𝑠𝑒_𝑝𝑟𝑜𝑔𝑟𝑎𝑚
+ “\n\nReturn ONLY the annotated Dafny code in a ```dafny code block.” + 𝑒𝑟𝑟; }
𝑓𝑑:= function 𝑓𝑑(𝑏𝑎𝑠𝑒_𝑝𝑟𝑜𝑔𝑟𝑎𝑚: 𝑠𝑡𝑟, 𝑦: 𝑠𝑡𝑟) : (𝑠𝑡𝑟)
ensures 𝑛𝑜𝐷𝑖𝑓𝑓(𝑏𝑎𝑠𝑒_𝑝𝑟𝑜𝑔𝑟𝑎𝑚, 𝑓𝑑(𝑏𝑎𝑠𝑒_𝑝𝑟𝑜𝑔𝑟𝑎𝑚, 𝑦))
{
return 𝑏𝑎𝑠𝑒_𝑝𝑟𝑜𝑔𝑟𝑎𝑚;
}

Fig. 4. FGGM definition for 𝑖𝑛𝑖𝑡𝑖𝑎𝑙𝐹𝐺𝐺𝑀. The prompting function builds context-specific guidance by

var 𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑣_𝑣𝑒𝑟_𝑒𝑟𝑟: 𝑠𝑡𝑟:= “”;
var 𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑣_𝑐𝑜𝑑𝑒: 𝑠𝑡𝑟:= “”;
while (𝑎𝑡𝑡𝑒𝑚𝑝𝑡< 𝑚𝑎𝑥_𝑎𝑡𝑡𝑒𝑚𝑝𝑡𝑠)
invariant 𝑛𝑜𝐷𝑖𝑓𝑓(𝑏𝑎𝑠𝑒_𝑝𝑟𝑜𝑔𝑟𝑎𝑚,𝑏𝑒𝑠𝑡)
decreases 𝑚𝑎𝑥_𝑎𝑡𝑡𝑒𝑚𝑝𝑡𝑠−𝑎𝑡𝑡𝑒𝑚𝑝𝑡
{
var 𝑦: 𝑠𝑡𝑟;
if (𝑎𝑡𝑡𝑒𝑚𝑝𝑡= 0) {
𝑦:= 𝑖𝑛𝑖𝑡𝑖𝑎𝑙𝐹𝐺𝐺𝑀(𝑏𝑎𝑠𝑒_𝑝𝑟𝑜𝑔𝑟𝑎𝑚);
} else {
if (𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑣_𝑑𝑖𝑓𝑓_𝑒𝑟𝑟≠“”) {
𝑦:= 𝑑𝑖𝑓𝑓𝐸𝑟𝑟𝑜𝑟𝐹𝐺𝐺𝑀(𝑏𝑎𝑠𝑒_𝑝𝑟𝑜𝑔𝑟𝑎𝑚, 𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑣_𝑑𝑖𝑓𝑓_𝑒𝑟𝑟, 𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑣_𝑐𝑜𝑑𝑒);
} else {
𝑦:= 𝑣𝑒𝑟𝑖𝑓𝑖𝑒𝑟𝐸𝑟𝑟𝑜𝑟𝐹𝐺𝐺𝑀(𝑏𝑎𝑠𝑒_𝑝𝑟𝑜𝑔𝑟𝑎𝑚, 𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑣_𝑣𝑒𝑟_𝑒𝑟𝑟, 𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑣_𝑐𝑜𝑑𝑒);
}
}
𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑣_𝑑𝑖𝑓𝑓_𝑒𝑟𝑟:= “”;
𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑣_𝑣𝑒𝑟_𝑒𝑟𝑟:= “”;
𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑣_𝑐𝑜𝑑𝑒:= 𝑦;
var 𝑑𝑖𝑓𝑓_𝑒𝑟𝑟: 𝑠𝑡𝑟:= 𝑛𝑜𝐷𝑖𝑓𝑓𝑊𝑖𝑡ℎ𝐸𝑟𝑟𝑜𝑟𝑀𝑠𝑔(𝑏𝑎𝑠𝑒_𝑝𝑟𝑜𝑔𝑟𝑎𝑚, 𝑦);
if (𝑑𝑖𝑓𝑓_𝑒𝑟𝑟= “”) {
var 𝑣_𝑒𝑟𝑟: 𝑠𝑡𝑟:= 𝑑𝑎𝑓𝑛𝑦𝑉𝑒𝑟𝑖𝑓𝑖𝑒𝑟𝑊𝑖𝑡ℎ𝐸𝑟𝑟𝑜𝑟𝑀𝑠𝑔(𝑦);
if (𝑣_𝑒𝑟𝑟= “”) {
𝑟:= 𝑦; return;
} else {
if (¬𝑏𝑒𝑠𝑡_𝑣𝑒𝑟𝑖𝑓𝑖𝑒𝑑) { 𝑏𝑒𝑠𝑡:= 𝑦; }
𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑣_𝑣𝑒𝑟_𝑒𝑟𝑟:= 𝑣_𝑒𝑟𝑟;
}
} else {

}
𝑎𝑡𝑡𝑒𝑚𝑝𝑡:= 𝑎𝑡𝑡𝑒𝑚𝑝𝑡+ 1;
}
𝑟:= 𝑏𝑒𝑠𝑡; return;
}

generates an initial annotation. On subsequent iterations, the agent dispatches to 𝑑𝑖𝑓𝑓𝐸𝑟𝑟𝑜𝑟𝐹𝐺𝐺𝑀
(Fig. 5) or 𝑣𝑒𝑟𝑖𝑓𝑖𝑒𝑟𝐸𝑟𝑟𝑜𝑟𝐹𝐺𝐺𝑀(Fig. 6) depending on the previous error type. Each FGGM guarantees
𝑛𝑜𝐷𝑖𝑓𝑓(𝑏𝑎𝑠𝑒_𝑝𝑟𝑜𝑔𝑟𝑎𝑚,𝑦) via a rejection sampler with 𝐾= 5 and stateful 𝐸𝑟𝑟Ψ𝑙feedback, maintaining
the loop invariant. The fallback fires on only 5.1% of instances (HumanEvalDafny) and 7.9% (DafnyBench),
confirming that the 𝐸𝑟𝑟Ψ𝑙-guided sampler produces conforming outputs in most cases. Upon exhausting all
attempts, the agent returns 𝑏𝑒𝑠𝑡, which satisfies the postcondition by the loop invariant.

Example Agent for GSM-Symbolic

#### The following is an example verified agent program for the GSM-Symbolic math synthesis task.


#### The agent defines and invokes four FGGMs, 𝑖𝑛𝑖𝑡𝑖𝑎𝑙𝐹𝐺𝐺𝑀(Fig. 8), 𝑝𝑎𝑟𝑠𝑒𝐸𝑟𝑟𝑜𝑟𝐹𝐺𝐺𝑀1 (Fig. 9),

𝑝𝑎𝑟𝑠𝑒𝐸𝑟𝑟𝑜𝑟𝐹𝐺𝐺𝑀2 (Fig. 10), and𝑝𝑎𝑟𝑠𝑒𝐸𝑟𝑟𝑜𝑟𝐹𝐺𝐺𝑀3 (Fig. 11), each with local contract Ψ𝑙: 𝑔𝑠𝑚𝑃𝑎𝑟𝑠𝑒𝑟(·)

#### enforcing syntactic validity of the output expression and a fallback that returns “0” (a trivially

parsable expression). All four share 𝐺𝑀𝑖𝑑:= 𝑞𝑤𝑒𝑛and differ in the prompting function 𝑓𝑝and out-
put processor 𝑓𝑝𝑜𝑠𝑡: the first generates an initial math expression, while the remaining three provide
increasingly focused repair strategies for parse errors. In each FGGM, 𝑓𝑝𝑜𝑠𝑡extracts the expression
wrapped in << >> from the raw model response, and the auto-synthesized stateful error function
𝐸𝑟𝑟Ψ𝑙(which reports the failing atomic formulas of Ψ𝑙, i.e., parse-failure details) is threaded back to
the prompt through its 𝑒𝑟𝑟argument, enabling the rejection sampler (Fig. 2) to avoid repeating the
same syntactic mistakes. The agent program (Fig. 12) first attempts generation via 𝑖𝑛𝑖𝑡𝑖𝑎𝑙𝐹𝐺𝐺𝑀,
then dispatches to the appropriate parse-error FGGM on subsequent iterations. On GSM-Symbolic,

#### the fallback fires on only 2.1% of instances (Table 8), confirming that the 𝐸𝑟𝑟Ψ𝑙-guided sampler

produces syntactically valid expressions in nearly all cases.

+ “1. Are all variable names exactly as listed?\n”
+ “2. Are you using only: +, -, *, /, //, %, **, int(), ()?\n”
+ “3. No round(), abs(), or other functions?\n\n”
+ “Corrected expression in << >>:”;
return 𝑝+ 𝑒𝑟𝑟; }

method 𝑎𝑔𝑒𝑛𝑡(𝑞𝑢𝑒𝑠𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛: 𝑠𝑡𝑟, 𝑣𝑎𝑟𝑖𝑎𝑏𝑙𝑒_𝑡𝑦𝑝𝑒𝑠: 𝑠𝑡𝑟) returns (𝑟: 𝑠𝑡𝑟)

var 𝑎𝑡𝑡𝑒𝑚𝑝𝑡: 𝑖𝑛𝑡:= 0;

// — repair loop —
while (𝑎𝑡𝑡𝑒𝑚𝑝𝑡< 𝑚𝑎𝑥_𝑎𝑡𝑡𝑒𝑚𝑝𝑡𝑠)
invariant 0 ≤𝑎𝑡𝑡𝑒𝑚𝑝𝑡≤𝑚𝑎𝑥_𝑎𝑡𝑡𝑒𝑚𝑝𝑡𝑠
decreases 𝑚𝑎𝑥_𝑎𝑡𝑡𝑒𝑚𝑝𝑡𝑠−𝑎𝑡𝑡𝑒𝑚𝑝𝑡
{
if (𝑟𝑒𝑠𝑢𝑙𝑡= “”) { 𝑟𝑒𝑠𝑢𝑙𝑡:= “0”; break; }
var 𝑝𝑎𝑟𝑠𝑒_𝑒𝑟𝑟𝑜𝑟: 𝑠𝑡𝑟:= 𝑔𝑠𝑚𝑃𝑎𝑟𝑠𝑒𝑟𝑊𝑖𝑡ℎ𝐸𝑟𝑟𝑜𝑟𝑀𝑠𝑔(𝑟𝑒𝑠𝑢𝑙𝑡);

𝑟:= 𝑟𝑒𝑠𝑢𝑙𝑡; return;
}
// — FGGM dispatch based on attempt —
𝑎𝑡𝑡𝑒𝑚𝑝𝑡:= 𝑎𝑡𝑡𝑒𝑚𝑝𝑡+ 1;
var 𝑓𝑖𝑥_𝑟𝑒𝑠𝑝𝑜𝑛𝑠𝑒: 𝑠𝑡𝑟;

} else if (𝑎𝑡𝑡𝑒𝑚𝑝𝑡= 2) {
𝑓𝑖𝑥_𝑟𝑒𝑠𝑝𝑜𝑛𝑠𝑒:= 𝑝𝑎𝑟𝑠𝑒𝐸𝑟𝑟𝑜𝑟𝐹𝐺𝐺𝑀2(𝑞𝑢𝑒𝑠𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛, 𝑣𝑎𝑟𝑖𝑎𝑏𝑙𝑒_𝑡𝑦𝑝𝑒𝑠, 𝑝𝑎𝑟𝑠𝑒_𝑒𝑟𝑟𝑜𝑟, 𝑟𝑒𝑠𝑢𝑙𝑡);

}

}
𝑟:= “0”; return;
}

M
Example Agent for 𝜏2-bench
The following is an example verified agent program for the 𝜏2-bench multi-turn tool-use task. The

#### agent defines and invokes three FGGMs, 𝑖𝑛𝑖𝑡𝑖𝑎𝑙𝐹𝐺𝐺𝑀(Fig. 13), 𝑑𝑢𝑝𝑙𝑖𝑐𝑎𝑡𝑒𝐹𝐺𝐺𝑀(Fig. 14), and

𝑎𝑔𝑒𝑛𝑡𝐶𝐸𝑟𝑟𝑜𝑟𝐹𝐺𝐺𝑀(Fig. 15), each of which returns an array of tool-call actions of type 𝑎𝑟𝑟⟨𝑠𝑡𝑟⟩

#### using the built-in array primitive, rather than a single action. Each FGGM has local contract


#### Ψ𝑙: 𝑎𝑔𝑒𝑛𝑡𝐶𝐶ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘𝐿𝑖𝑠𝑡(·, 𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑐𝑒, 𝑑𝑜𝑚𝑎𝑖𝑛), a sequence-level checker ensuring that every action in

the generated array satisfies the Agent-C temporal policy given the trace extended by the preceding
actions. Here 𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑐𝑒and 𝑑𝑜𝑚𝑎𝑖𝑛denote the running tool-call trace and the domain identifier (the
𝑡𝑜𝑜𝑙_𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑐𝑒and 𝑑𝑜𝑚𝑎𝑖𝑛parameters of the agent method in Fig. 16); they are ambient contract inputs
threaded to every FGGM by the framework, so the 𝑡𝑦𝑝𝑒𝑆𝑖𝑔lists only the task-specific arguments.
The fallback returns a singleton array that transfers to a human agent, which trivially satisfies the

#### policy. All three share 𝐺𝑀𝑖𝑑:= 𝑞𝑤𝑒𝑛and differ in prompting function 𝑓𝑝and output processor

𝑓𝑝𝑜𝑠𝑡: the first generates an initial action array, the second handles duplicate action-array detection,
and the third recovers from Agent-C constraint violations with targeted guidance. In each FGGM,
𝑓𝑝𝑜𝑠𝑡parses the ordered array of tool calls from the raw model response, while the auto-synthesized
stateful error function 𝐸𝑟𝑟Ψ𝑙(which reports the failing atomic formulas of Ψ𝑙, i.e., policy-violation
details) is threaded back to the prompt through its 𝑒𝑟𝑟argument, enabling the rejection sampler

#### (Fig. 2) to avoid repeating the same policy-violating action arrays. The agent program (Fig. 16)

dispatches to the appropriate FGGM based on duplicate detection and constraint-check results. On
𝜏2-bench, the fallback fires on 15.6% (retail) and 21.6% (airline) of instances (Table 8).
, Vol. 1, No. 1, Article . Publication date: October 2026.

+ “\n\nCRITICAL INSTRUCTIONS:\n”
+ “1. When user confirms an action, EXECUTE it immediately with the correct tool call\n”
+ “2. For reservation updates: call update_reservation_flights with NEW flights\n”
+ “3. For basic economy changes: cancel_reservation then book_reservation\n”
+ “4. Always communicate specific numbers: prices, refund amounts, IDs\n”
+ “5. When no direct flights exist, try search_onestop_flight\n”
+ “6. Only transfer to human after genuinely attempting required actions\n”
+ “7. Emit the full ordered array of tool-call actions to take this turn\n”;
return “[system: ” + 𝑠𝑦𝑠+ “] ” + 𝑐𝑜𝑛𝑣𝑒𝑟𝑠𝑎𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛+ 𝑒𝑟𝑟; }
𝑓𝑑:= function 𝑓𝑑(𝑐𝑜𝑛𝑣𝑒𝑟𝑠𝑎𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛: 𝑠𝑡𝑟, 𝑝𝑜𝑙𝑖𝑐𝑦: 𝑠𝑡𝑟, 𝑡𝑜𝑜𝑙𝑠: 𝑠𝑡𝑟, 𝑦: 𝑎𝑟𝑟⟨𝑠𝑡𝑟⟩) : (𝑎𝑟𝑟⟨𝑠𝑡𝑟⟩)
ensures 𝑎𝑔𝑒𝑛𝑡𝐶𝐶ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘𝐿𝑖𝑠𝑡(𝑓𝑑(. . . ), 𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑐𝑒, 𝑑𝑜𝑚𝑎𝑖𝑛)
{
return [𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑛𝑠𝑓𝑒𝑟𝑇𝑜𝐻𝑢𝑚𝑎𝑛(“Unable to process request”)];
}
𝑓𝑝𝑜𝑠𝑡:= function 𝑓𝑝𝑜𝑠𝑡(𝑦𝑟𝑎𝑤: 𝑠𝑡𝑟) : (𝑎𝑟𝑟⟨𝑠𝑡𝑟⟩) {
return 𝑝𝑎𝑟𝑠𝑒𝑇𝑜𝑜𝑙𝐶𝑎𝑙𝑙𝑠(𝑦𝑟𝑎𝑤); }
Fig. 13. FGGM definition for𝑖𝑛𝑖𝑡𝑖𝑎𝑙𝐹𝐺𝐺𝑀(𝜏2-bench). The FGGM emits an array of tool-call actions (𝑎𝑟𝑟⟨𝑠𝑡𝑟⟩),
and Ψ𝑙uses the sequence-level checker 𝑎𝑔𝑒𝑛𝑡𝐶𝐶ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘𝐿𝑖𝑠𝑡that verifies every action complies with the Agent-C
policy given the trace extended by the preceding actions. The prompting function provides domain-specific
instructions and appends the auto-synthesized rejection feedback 𝑒𝑟𝑟, enabling the rejection sampler to avoid
repeating non-compliant action arrays. The output processor 𝑓𝑝𝑜𝑠𝑡parses the ordered array of tool calls from
the raw model response, and the fallback returns a singleton array that transfers to a human agent. Fallback
trigger rate: 15.6% retail, 21.6% airline (Table 8).

𝑡𝑜𝑜𝑙_𝑛𝑎𝑚𝑒: 𝑠𝑡𝑟, 𝑡𝑜𝑜𝑙𝑠: 𝑠𝑡𝑟, 𝑒𝑟𝑟: 𝑠𝑡𝑟) : (𝑠𝑡𝑟) {

+ “’ failed agentC constraint: ” + 𝑎𝑔𝑒𝑛𝑡𝑐_𝑒𝑟𝑟𝑜𝑟+ “\n\n”;
if (𝑖𝑠𝑆𝑢𝑏𝑠𝑡𝑟𝑖𝑛𝑔(“update_reservation”, 𝑡𝑜𝑜𝑙_𝑛𝑎𝑚𝑒)) {

+ “2. Check if cabin is basic_economy – if yes, cancel then book.\n”
+ “3. For flight changes: search for new flights first.\n”
+ “Try calling the missing prerequisite tool now.\n”; }

+ “Verify cancellation rules are satisfied.\n”; }

𝑔𝑢𝑖𝑑𝑎𝑛𝑐𝑒:= 𝑔𝑢𝑖𝑑𝑎𝑛𝑐𝑒
+ “To book: call get_user_details for payment methods,\n”
+ “search for flights, ensure all passenger details.\n”; }
else {
𝑔𝑢𝑖𝑑𝑎𝑛𝑐𝑒:= 𝑔𝑢𝑖𝑑𝑎𝑛𝑐𝑒
+ “Call prerequisite tools first: get_user_details, get_reservation_details, or search flights.\n”; }
return 𝑚𝑒𝑠𝑠𝑎𝑔𝑒𝑠+ “ + [assistant: ” + 𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑣_𝑟𝑒𝑠𝑝𝑜𝑛𝑠𝑒
+ “, user: ” + 𝑔𝑢𝑖𝑑𝑎𝑛𝑐𝑒+ “]” + 𝑒𝑟𝑟; }

SEVerA : Verified Self-Evolving Agents with Specification Guidance
47
method 𝑎𝑔𝑒𝑛𝑡(𝑐𝑜𝑛𝑣𝑒𝑟𝑠𝑎𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛: 𝑠𝑡𝑟, 𝑡𝑜𝑜𝑙_𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑐𝑒: 𝑠𝑡𝑟, 𝑎𝑣𝑎𝑖𝑙𝑎𝑏𝑙𝑒_𝑡𝑜𝑜𝑙𝑠: 𝑠𝑡𝑟,
𝑝𝑜𝑙𝑖𝑐𝑦: 𝑠𝑡𝑟, 𝑑𝑜𝑚𝑎𝑖𝑛: 𝑠𝑡𝑟, 𝑡𝑜𝑜𝑙𝑠: 𝑠𝑡𝑟) returns (𝑟: 𝑎𝑟𝑟⟨𝑠𝑡𝑟⟩)
ensures 𝑎𝑔𝑒𝑛𝑡𝐶𝐶ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘𝐿𝑖𝑠𝑡(𝑟, 𝑡𝑜𝑜𝑙_𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑐𝑒, 𝑑𝑜𝑚𝑎𝑖𝑛)
{
var 𝑚𝑎𝑥_𝑟𝑒𝑡𝑟𝑖𝑒𝑠: 𝑖𝑛𝑡:= 5;
var 𝑟𝑒𝑡𝑟𝑦_𝑐𝑜𝑢𝑛𝑡: 𝑖𝑛𝑡:= 0;
var 𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑣_𝑎𝑐𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛𝑠: 𝑎𝑟𝑟⟨𝑠𝑡𝑟⟩:= [];
var 𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑣_𝑟𝑒𝑠𝑝𝑜𝑛𝑠𝑒: 𝑠𝑡𝑟:= “”;
// — initial FGGM call: generate an array of actions —
var 𝑎𝑐𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛𝑠: 𝑎𝑟𝑟⟨𝑠𝑡𝑟⟩:= 𝑖𝑛𝑖𝑡𝑖𝑎𝑙𝐹𝐺𝐺𝑀(𝑐𝑜𝑛𝑣𝑒𝑟𝑠𝑎𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛, 𝑝𝑜𝑙𝑖𝑐𝑦, 𝑡𝑜𝑜𝑙𝑠);
// — main loop —
while (𝑟𝑒𝑡𝑟𝑦_𝑐𝑜𝑢𝑛𝑡< 𝑚𝑎𝑥_𝑟𝑒𝑡𝑟𝑖𝑒𝑠)
decreases 𝑚𝑎𝑥_𝑟𝑒𝑡𝑟𝑖𝑒𝑠−𝑟𝑒𝑡𝑟𝑦_𝑐𝑜𝑢𝑛𝑡
{
// — duplicate detection over the whole action array —
if (𝑎𝑐𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛𝑠= 𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑣_𝑎𝑐𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛𝑠) {
𝑟𝑒𝑡𝑟𝑦_𝑐𝑜𝑢𝑛𝑡:= 𝑟𝑒𝑡𝑟𝑦_𝑐𝑜𝑢𝑛𝑡+ 1;
if (𝑟𝑒𝑡𝑟𝑦_𝑐𝑜𝑢𝑛𝑡≥𝑚𝑎𝑥_𝑟𝑒𝑡𝑟𝑖𝑒𝑠) {
𝑟:= [𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑛𝑠𝑓𝑒𝑟𝑇𝑜𝐻𝑢𝑚𝑎𝑛(“Unable to proceed.”)]; return;
}
𝑎𝑐𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛𝑠:= 𝑑𝑢𝑝𝑙𝑖𝑐𝑎𝑡𝑒𝐹𝐺𝐺𝑀(𝑐𝑜𝑛𝑣𝑒𝑟𝑠𝑎𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛, 𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑣_𝑟𝑒𝑠𝑝𝑜𝑛𝑠𝑒, 𝑓𝑖𝑟𝑠𝑡𝑇𝑜𝑜𝑙𝑁𝑎𝑚𝑒(𝑎𝑐𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛𝑠), 𝑡𝑜𝑜𝑙𝑠);
continue;
}
𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑣_𝑎𝑐𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛𝑠:= 𝑎𝑐𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛𝑠;
𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑣_𝑟𝑒𝑠𝑝𝑜𝑛𝑠𝑒:= 𝑗𝑜𝑖𝑛𝐴𝑐𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛𝑠(𝑎𝑐𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛𝑠);
// — sequence-level agentC constraint check —
var 𝑒𝑟𝑟𝑜𝑟_𝑚𝑠𝑔: 𝑠𝑡𝑟:=
𝑎𝑔𝑒𝑛𝑡𝐶𝐶ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘𝐿𝑖𝑠𝑡𝑊𝑖𝑡ℎ𝐸𝑟𝑟𝑜𝑟𝑀𝑠𝑔(𝑎𝑐𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛𝑠, 𝑡𝑜𝑜𝑙_𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑐𝑒, 𝑑𝑜𝑚𝑎𝑖𝑛);
if (𝑒𝑟𝑟𝑜𝑟_𝑚𝑠𝑔= “”) {
𝑟:= 𝑎𝑐𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛𝑠; return;
} else {
𝑟𝑒𝑡𝑟𝑦_𝑐𝑜𝑢𝑛𝑡:= 𝑟𝑒𝑡𝑟𝑦_𝑐𝑜𝑢𝑛𝑡+ 1;
if (𝑟𝑒𝑡𝑟𝑦_𝑐𝑜𝑢𝑛𝑡≥𝑚𝑎𝑥_𝑟𝑒𝑡𝑟𝑖𝑒𝑠) {
𝑟:= [𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑛𝑠𝑓𝑒𝑟𝑇𝑜𝐻𝑢𝑚𝑎𝑛(“Constraint failures. Error: ” + 𝑒𝑟𝑟𝑜𝑟_𝑚𝑠𝑔)]; return;
}
𝑎𝑐𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛𝑠:= 𝑎𝑔𝑒𝑛𝑡𝐶𝐸𝑟𝑟𝑜𝑟𝐹𝐺𝐺𝑀(𝑐𝑜𝑛𝑣𝑒𝑟𝑠𝑎𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛, 𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑣_𝑟𝑒𝑠𝑝𝑜𝑛𝑠𝑒, 𝑒𝑟𝑟𝑜𝑟_𝑚𝑠𝑔,
𝑓𝑖𝑟𝑠𝑡𝑇𝑜𝑜𝑙𝑁𝑎𝑚𝑒(𝑎𝑐𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛𝑠), 𝑡𝑜𝑜𝑙𝑠);
continue;
}
}
// — exhausted retries —
𝑟:= [𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑛𝑠𝑓𝑒𝑟𝑇𝑜𝐻𝑢𝑚𝑎𝑛(“Unable to process request after multiple attempts”)]; return;
}
Fig. 16. Verified agent program for 𝜏2-bench. The agent generates an array of tool-call actions via
𝑖𝑛𝑖𝑡𝑖𝑎𝑙𝐹𝐺𝐺𝑀, then enters a loop that detects duplicate action arrays (dispatching to 𝑑𝑢𝑝𝑙𝑖𝑐𝑎𝑡𝑒𝐹𝐺𝐺𝑀)
and checks sequence-level Agent-C policy compliance with 𝑎𝑔𝑒𝑛𝑡𝐶𝐶ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘𝐿𝑖𝑠𝑡𝑊𝑖𝑡ℎ𝐸𝑟𝑟𝑜𝑟𝑀𝑠𝑔(dispatching
to 𝑎𝑔𝑒𝑛𝑡𝐶𝐸𝑟𝑟𝑜𝑟𝐹𝐺𝐺𝑀on failure). Each FGGM returns a policy-compliant action array (𝑎𝑟𝑟⟨𝑠𝑡𝑟⟩) via a re-
jection sampler with 𝐾= 5 and stateful 𝐸𝑟𝑟Ψ𝑙feedback, so the returned array satisfies the postcondition
𝑎𝑔𝑒𝑛𝑡𝐶𝐶ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘𝐿𝑖𝑠𝑡(𝑟, 𝑡𝑜𝑜𝑙_𝑡𝑟𝑎𝑐𝑒, 𝑑𝑜𝑚𝑎𝑖𝑛). The fallback returns a singleton transfer-to-human array and fires
on 15.6% (retail) and 21.6% (airline) of instances (Table 8).

FGGM Syntax
The syntax of a first-order guarded generative model (FGGM) can be described using the follow-
ing BNF grammar. We use the production rules (⟨ident⟩, ⟨type⟩, ⟨program⟩, ⟨𝑞𝑓_𝑓𝑜𝑟𝑚𝑢𝑙𝑎⟩) from
Appendix A.
⟨𝑖𝑑⟩::= ⟨ident⟩
⟨𝐺𝑀𝑖𝑑⟩::= ⟨ident⟩
⟨𝑡𝑦𝑝𝑒𝑆𝑖𝑔⟩::= ”(”⟨typedVars⟩”)”” →”⟨type⟩
⟨typedVars⟩::= ⟨typedVar⟩| ⟨typedVar⟩, ⟨typedVars⟩
⟨typedVar⟩::= ⟨ident⟩: ⟨type⟩
⟨Φ𝑙⟩::= "requires" ⟨𝑞𝑓_𝑓𝑜𝑟𝑚𝑢𝑙𝑎⟩
⟨Ψ𝑙⟩::= "ensures" ⟨𝑞𝑓_𝑓𝑜𝑟𝑚𝑢𝑙𝑎⟩
⟨𝑓𝑝⟩::= ⟨program⟩
⟨𝑓𝑑⟩::= ⟨program⟩
⟨𝑓𝑝𝑜𝑠𝑡⟩::= ⟨program⟩
O
FGGM Validity Check
Algorithm 3 checks whether an FGGM definition is valid. It verifies that the type signature and local
contracts are syntactically well-formed and that all terms using library functions satisfy their input
specifications. It then checks that the prompting program and fallback program are type-correct

#### and terminating. Finally, the fallback program is verified using the deductive verifier to ensure

it satisfies the local contract for all inputs. The FGGM definition is accepted only if no errors are
found.
, Vol. 1, No. 1, Article . Publication date: October 2026.

resulting formula by recursively computing all terms using the library functions.

Background on GRPO
Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO): GRPO is a reinforcement learning–based fine-
tuning method for generative models that optimizes a policy by comparing outputs relative to other
samples from the same input, rather than relying on an explicit value function. Let 𝜋𝜃(𝑦| 𝑝) denote

#### the parametric policy for an FGGM 𝑖𝑑𝜃𝑖, where 𝑝∈P is the input prompt and 𝑦𝑙∼𝜋𝜃(· | 𝑝) is a

sampled output. For each input 𝑝, GRPO samples a group of 𝐺candidate outputs {𝑦(1), . . . ,𝑦(𝐺)}
from the current policy. Each sample is assigned a scalar reward 𝑟(𝑗) = R𝑎(𝑝,𝑦(𝑗)) (the per-sample

#### augmented reward of Eq. 23, defined below). Instead of using absolute rewards directly, GRPO

computes relative advantages within the group:
𝐴(𝑗) = 𝑟(𝑗) −𝜇𝑝
𝜎𝑝

Here 𝜇𝑝and 𝜎𝑝are the mean and standard deviation of rewards within the group, and 𝜉> 0 is a
small numerical-stability constant.

#### where 𝜌(𝑗) =

𝜋𝜃(𝑦(𝑗) |𝑝)

#### 𝜋𝜃old (𝑦(𝑗) |𝑝) is the importance ratio and 𝜖clip is the PPO clip range. This clipped

objective, similar to PPO, ensures stable policy updates. In our setting, each FGGM 𝑖𝑑𝜃𝑖defines a
policy 𝜋𝜃𝑖(𝑦𝑙| 𝑝) over outputs𝑦𝑙for input 𝑝, and the per-sample reward 𝑟(𝑗) above is the augmented
reward of Eq. 23, restated here:
R𝑎(𝑝,𝑦𝑙) = log(R(𝑥, _,𝑦) × I(𝑦= 𝑦𝑙) + 𝜖) + log(𝜆× I(𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘Ψ𝑙(𝑝,𝑦𝑙)) + 𝜖)
(26)

#### This reward combines two signals. The task term R(𝑥, _,𝑦) × I(𝑦= 𝑦𝑙) rewards outputs 𝑦𝑙that

determine the final agent output 𝑦and achieve high task reward; for intermediate outputs (𝑦𝑙≠𝑦)
it vanishes, ensuring proper credit assignment. The conformance term I(𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘Ψ𝑙(𝑝,𝑦𝑙)), scaled by

#### 𝜆, rewards outputs that satisfy the local contract (Φ𝑙, Ψ𝑙), thereby increasing rejection-sampling


#### acceptance. The constant 𝜖> 0 is the same log-smoothing constant as in Eq. 23. Because GRPO

only requires sampling from 𝜋𝜃𝑖and scalar rewards, the non-differentiable checker 𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘Ψ𝑙poses
no obstacle. Overall, GRPO optimizes each FGGM policy 𝜋𝜃𝑖to increase the likelihood of outputs
that (i) minimize downstream task loss when relevant and (ii) satisfy local contracts, aligning the
learned generative behavior with the decomposed objective in Eq. 23.

After each search–verify–learn iteration, SEVerA constructs structured feedback 𝐼′ from the execu-
tion traces of the current best candidate agent on the training data D (the collectFeedback step of
Algorithm 2). The planner LLM receives the task-performance score of the previous program along
with per-example error diagnostics. Specifically, for each training example on which the previous
candidate failed, SEVerA provides the planner with the example input, the agent’s output, and the
corresponding error message (e.g., a verification error, a grammar violation, or an incorrect answer).

#### The planner is then asked to generate a one-to-two sentence description and a suggested fix for


#### each failed example. The resulting list of per-example descriptions and suggestions is collected

and fed back to the planner as part of the context 𝐼′ when it samples the next candidate program.
This feedback mechanism enables the planner to identify recurring failure patterns and adjust its
synthesized FGGM definitions and prompting programs 𝑓𝑝accordingly in subsequent iterations.
T
Planner LLM Prompt

#### The following is the prompt template used by the planner LLM to synthesize candidate agent

programs. Placeholders in braces (e.g., {task_description}) are populated at each iteration with
the task specification, postcondition, available operator library, agent signature, and feedback from
the previous iteration’s execution traces (Appendix S).

- Check the postcondition before every return

- Predefine variables before using them to avoid syntax errors

V
FGGM Expressivity Preservation
A natural concern with any post-hoc constraint enforcement mechanism is whether it implicitly
limits the computational power of the underlying model. In particular, naive constrained decoding
methods that restrict every generated token to conform to a finite output grammar can collapse the
effective expressivity of constant-depth transformers to the circuit complexity class DLOGTIME-
uniform 𝑇𝐶0 (constant-depth, polynomial-size threshold circuits) [4]. Since 𝑇𝐶0 is believed to be

#### strictly weaker than 𝑁𝐿(nondeterministic log-space), such approaches provably cannot solve

problems like graph reachability under standard complexity-theoretic assumptions. We show that
the FGGM construction avoids this expressivity loss entirely.
Expressivity of transformers with chain-of-thought. A series of works [31, 33] establish that
constant-depth, log-precision transformers without intermediate reasoning steps (i.e., producing a
single autoregressive output) can be simulated by logspace-uniform 𝑇𝐶0 circuits. However, when
allowed to produce𝑡(𝑛) intermediate autoregressive steps, where𝑡(𝑛) is polynomial in the input size
, Vol. 1, No. 1, Article . Publication date: October 2026.

SEVerA : Verified Self-Evolving Agents with Specification Guidance
57
𝑛, the same class of transformers can simulate any Turing machine 𝑀that halts within 𝑡(𝑛) steps.
The construction from [33] works as follows: each autoregressive step encodes one configuration

#### of 𝑀, capturing the current state 𝑞∈𝑄, the symbols at each tape head, and the head movement

directions. Since the set of states 𝑄, the tape alphabet Γ, and the number of tapes are all constants,

#### the total number of distinct configuration encodings is also constant, and hence representable


#### by a finite vocabulary 𝑉𝑀. The LLM L𝑀then outputs the sequence of configuration encodings


#### 𝛾1 · · ·𝛾𝑡(𝑛) followed by the final output 𝑀(𝑥), effectively unrolling the entire computation of 𝑀

across autoregressive steps. We restate this result as Lemma V.1.
Lemma V.1 (Expressivity of CoT [33]). For any Turing machine 𝑀with tape alphabet Γ, there
exists a constant-depth LLM L𝑀with finite vocabulary 𝑉𝑀⊇Γ and log-precision that can simulate
𝑡(𝑛) steps of 𝑀using 𝑡(𝑛) autoregressive steps, where 𝑡(𝑛) is polynomial in the input size 𝑛. For any

#### input 𝑥with |𝑥| = 𝑛on which 𝑀halts within 𝑡(𝑛) steps, the unconstrained output takes the form

L𝑀(𝑥) = 𝛾1 · · ·𝛾𝑡(𝑛) · 𝑀(𝑥) where 𝛾1, . . . ,𝛾𝑡(𝑛) ∈𝑉𝑀are encodings of the machine’s configurations
at each computational step.
Why FGGM preserves expressivity. The key observation is that the underlying GM in an FGGM
generates its output unconstrained — no token-level grammar mask is applied and no bound on
generation length is imposed during the generation process. This allows the GM to use arbitrarily
many intermediate chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning steps. The post-processor 𝑓𝑝𝑜𝑠𝑡then extracts
the final answer from the raw GM output using the built-in 𝑠𝑝𝑙𝑖𝑡function (§ 5.1) with an appropriate
delimiter character 𝑐, and the rejection sampler checks only the extracted answer against the local
contract Ψ𝑙. Since the reasoning tokens are stripped before any constraint checking, they place no
restriction on the model’s intermediate computation. Consequently, the FGGM retains the full CoT
expressivity established by Lemma V.1. We formalize this as Theorem 5.11.
Theorem 5.11 (FGGM Expressivity). For any Turing machine 𝑀with tape alphabet Γ and any
valid FGGM G with quantifier-free local contract (Φ𝑙, Ψ𝑙), if 𝑀computes outputs satisfying Ψ𝑙on all
inputs satisfying Φ𝑙, then there exists a constant-depth LLM L𝑀with log-precision, a delimiter 𝑐∉Γ,
and a post-processor 𝑓𝑝𝑜𝑠𝑡using 𝑠𝑝𝑙𝑖𝑡(𝑐, ·) such that for any input 𝑥with |𝑥| = 𝑛, the FGGM with GM
L𝑀returns 𝑀(𝑥) without triggering the fallback, assuming 𝑀halts on 𝑥within 𝑡(𝑛) steps where 𝑡(𝑛)
is polynomial in 𝑛.

#### Proof. Let 𝑀be an arbitrary Turing machine with tape alphabet Γ that halts on all inputs

satisfying Φ𝑙within 𝑡(𝑛) steps, where 𝑡(𝑛) is polynomial in 𝑛= |𝑥|.

#### Step 1: Constructing the LLM. By Lemma V.1, there exists a constant-depth LLM L𝑀with

vocabulary𝑉𝑀⊇Γ and log-precision such that on any input𝑥, L𝑀(𝑥) = 𝛾1 · · ·𝛾𝑡(𝑛) ·𝑀(𝑥). Choose

#### a fresh delimiter symbol 𝑐∉𝑉𝑀and extend the vocabulary to 𝑉′


#### 𝑀= 𝑉𝑀∪{𝑐}. We modify L𝑀to

produce L′
𝑀(𝑥) = 𝛾1 · · ·𝛾𝑡(𝑛) · 𝑐· 𝑀(𝑥). This requires one additional autoregressive step to output
𝑐before the final answer, preserving the constant-depth property. Since all configuration encodings
𝛾𝑖∈𝑉𝑀and 𝑐∉𝑉𝑀, the delimiter 𝑐does not appear in the reasoning prefix.
Step 2: Defining the post-processor. Define 𝑓𝑝𝑜𝑠𝑡(𝑦) = 𝑠𝑝𝑙𝑖𝑡(𝑐,𝑦)[|𝑠𝑝𝑙𝑖𝑡(𝑐,𝑦)| −1], which returns
the substring after the last occurrence of 𝑐. Since 𝑐appears exactly once in L′
𝑀(𝑥) (separating the
reasoning from the final answer), 𝑓𝑝𝑜𝑠𝑡(L′
𝑀(𝑥)) = 𝑀(𝑥).

#### Step 3: FGGM acceptance. Consider the FGGM G with local contract (Φ𝑙, Ψ𝑙), underlying GM

L′

#### 𝑀(𝑓𝑝(𝑥1, . . . ,𝑥𝑛))) = 𝑀(𝑥).

By assumption, Ψ𝑙(𝑥1, . . . ,𝑥𝑛, 𝑀(𝑥)) holds. Since Ψ𝑙is quantifier-free, by completeness of 𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘Ψ𝑙
(Lemma 5.3), 𝑐ℎ𝑒𝑐𝑘Ψ𝑙(𝑥1, . . . ,𝑥𝑛, 𝑀(𝑥)) returns true. Therefore, the rejection sampler accepts 𝑀(𝑥)
on the first sample, the fallback is never triggered, and the FGGM returns 𝑀(𝑥).
